uniform samples from
[-7,-1]
.
At each run, we observe a sequence
\{Y_{n}\}
with
Y_{n}=X(t_{n})+\sigma\varepsilon_{n}
for
\varepsilon_{n}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{p})
. The additive noise sequence
\{\varepsilon_{n}\}
is different across runs while
X(t)
is the same. For each run and given fixed
\lambda
, we obtain
\widehat{E}^{\Xi(\ell)}
, the estimated edge set, where
\Xi
is the optimal permutation of the index set
\{1,\ldots,k\}
. We proceed to compute the true positive rate (TPR) and false positive rate (FPR). The TPR of state
\ell
is

\text{TPR}(\ell)=\frac{|\{(i,j):(i,j)\in\widehat{E}^{\Xi(\ell)}\}\cap\{(i,j):(i,j)\in E^{\ell}\}|}{|E^{\ell}|}.

Similarly, the FPR of state
\ell
is defined as

\text{FPR}(\ell)=\frac{|\{(i,j):(i,j)\in\widehat{E}^{\Xi(\ell)}\}-\{(i,j):(i,j)\in E^{\ell}\}|}{p^{2}-|E^{\ell}|}.

Finally, we collect the TPR and FPR for each eligible outcome, the result associated with a
\lambda
such that
\Xi_{1}\equiv\Xi_{2}
and plot the ROC curve for each run.

Given fixed time interval, we demonstrate the performance of the algorithm under different sampling frequencies, resulting in different number of sample size. We experiment with
T=40,80,120,160,200
. The results are presented in Figure
3
–
4
and Table
1
–
2
. The oracle method assumes that the latent process
Z(t)
is given, and hence we do not need to compute the E-step. Figure
3
demonstrates the results running Algorithm
1
with
\widehat{X}(t)
estimated using the method developed in Section
3.1
. In contrast, Figure
4
demonstrates the results running Algorithm
1
with true
{X}(t)
. Perhaps not surprisingly, under small sample size, i.e.,
N=40,80,120
, the proposed method has higher AUC, shown in Table
1
–
2
, if
X(t)
is given. However, under larger sample size, i.e.,
N=160,180
, there is no big difference between using
\widehat{X}(t)
or
X(t)
. Similar conclusion also holds for the oracle method. When comparing the proposed method to the oracle method, we can see that the oracle method has higher AUC given fixed sample size. This is not surprising as getting the estimated smoothing probability close to the true probability is challenging under small sample size.

Figure 3

The average ROC of the simulated tasks over
10
independent runs with
X(t)
estimated using the procedure developed in Section
3.1
.
Top row.
Results of the proposed method. The AUC consistently increases as the sample size increases for all for graphs.

Bottom row.
Results of the oracle method, where the latent process
Z(t)
is assumed to be known. When the latent state is known, the AUC is larger compared to the AUC of the proposed method given same sample size.

6
Experiments

We apply the proposed model to characterize Attention-Deficit/Hyperactivity Disorder (ADHD), one of the complex neurological disorders developed in early childhood. Specifically, we focus on finding the group differences of brain networks from Typically Developed Children (TDC) and ADHD-combined (ADHD-C) type, a common subtype of ADHD that presents both inattentiveness and hyperactivity/impulsivity.

Shappell et al. (2021)
;
Park et al. (2021)
have found that resting-state brain networks of ADHD-C and TDC exhibit distinct group differences in connectivity states and transition rates.
Shappell et al. (2021)
modeled the resting-state fMRI as an HMM with independent Gaussian emissions, and both ADHD and TDC patients share the same graphs.
The results indicate that ADHD patients spend more time in the hyperconnected state and less time in anticorrelated states compared to TDC. Motivated by these findings, we propose to model real-world data by estimating shared graphs
\{\theta_{ij}^{\ell}\}
with group-specific transition rate matrices:
Q_{\text{ADHD}}
and
Q_{\text{TDC}}
. To analyze the difference of
Q_{\text{ADHD}}
and
Q_{\text{TDC}}
, we can compare the average dwell time differences at state
\ell
for
\ell=1,\ldots,k
.

We analyze the resting-state fMRI from NYU ADHD dataset
(
Castellanos et al., 2008
)
released in the ADHD
200
Initiative
(
Bellec et al., 2017
)
. We use the standard Athena preprocessing pipeline
(
Bellec et al., 2017
)
and select the subjects that pass the quality control test. To mitigate the age effects contributed to the development of ADHD, we select subjects within the age range from
7
to
10
following the criterion discussed in
(
Park et al., 2021
)
. Then, we parcellate the time-series using the Automated Anatomical atLas (AAL)
(
Tzourio-Mazoyer et al., 2002
)
, which has
116
regions of interest
(p=116)
. Each session has
N=172
recorded time points uniformly sampled within a
6
-minute time frame. Thus, for each test subject, we have
172
sample points to estimate graphs of size
p^{2}\times m\times k
, resulting in unreliable estimates. Motivated by the prior method
(
Shappell et al., 2021
)
, we concatenate the time-series ens employ a joint estimator. Specifically, we concatenate
15
TDC subjects and
15
ADHD-C subjects, resulting in a time-series of length
5160
. In the estimation step, we modify the proposed algorithm to estimate two
Q_{\text{ADHD}}
and
Q_{\text{TDC}}
in the M-step and then use the estimated
Q_{\text{ADHD}}
and
Q_{\text{TDC}}
to compute the latent probability at E-step separately. For model selection, we search the optimal number of states from
\{2,3,4,5,6\}
and find the optimal
\lambda
from the exponential
10
uniform samples within the interval of
[-6,-9]
. For parsimony and ease of interpretation, we use the linear basis function.

After model selection, we obtain
3
as the optimal number of states, and the optimal
\lambda
is
3.355\times 10^{-4}
.
The results are shown in Figure
5
.
To compare the differences between the ADHD-C group and TDC group, we calculate the average dwell time of the subjects at each state. This is calculated as

{\tau}_{\ell,\text{group}}=\frac{1}{{n_{\text{group}}}}\sum_{u=1}^{n_{\text{group}}}\tau_{\ell}\left(\{Y^{(u)}_{n}\}_{n=\{0,\ldots,N\}};Q_{\text{group}},\{\theta_{ij}^{\ell}\}\right),

where the group is either ADHD-C or TDC. The formula of
\tau_{\ell}
is defined in (
3.8
). The result in Table
3
indicates that ADHD-C group spends significantly more time at state
1
, while the TDC group spends significantly more time at state
2
. Both groups spend a comparable amount of time at state
3
, which qualitatively matches previous observations of dwell time differences across groups
Shappell et al. (2021)
;
Park et al. (2021)
.

Connectome of state
1
.

(b)

Connectome of state

.

Connectome of state
3
.

(d)

Transition rate matrix.
Left
:
Q_{\text{TDC}}
.

:

.

of each subject. The x-axis is the time point and the y-axis is accumulated probability that sums to

. Left column shows the probability maps of all subject from TDC group and right column shows the probability maps of all subject from ADHD-C group.

7
Discussion

Given the increasing interest in modeling real-world stochastic processes, which are more complicated than stationary and linear structures, we provide a more flexible framework to model the complex interactions with guarantees and provide the statistical guarantees of the algorithm. There are several interesting directions for further exploration.
Our approach assumes the slow-switching nature of the latent process and proposes an approximation procedure. An interesting direction would be studying how the transition rate, the values of
Q
, versus the observed sampling rate, affects the estimation quality. Under the high switching rate, one potential remedy is to integrate a second data modality that features a much higher sampling frequency. Motivated by the technological advances in the biomedical domain, we are able to obtain concurrent measurements of fMRI and EEG data, where EEG data has a much higher sampling frequency than that of fMRI. FMRI, in contrast, features high spatial resolution compared to EEG
(
Debener et al., 2006
)
, enabling us to obtain a fine-grained graph from the brain. Hence, one direction for future work is to use one modal (EEG) to uncover the hidden process and another modal (fMRI) to estimate the graph. The question is, then, how to conduct faithful joint estimations. Additionally, we model an ordinary differential process with additive observed noise. One direction to pursue is to understand under what conditions, graph recovery is feasible when the observations are generated from stochastic differential equations. This enables a broader understanding of causal structures from data generated from dynamical systems
(
Hansen and Sokol, 2014
;
Mogensen et al., 2018
)
.

Appendix A
Tail bound of the Wavelet Shrinkage

This section discusses the proof of Proposition
4.1
. We begin with stating the main proof, followed by auxiliary lemmas.

Proof of Proposition
4.1
.

The proof is inspired by Theorem 3 in
Brown and Cai (1998)
, and we extend the concentration result on the expectation to the tail bound.

Here, we drop the index
i=1,\ldots,p
of
X_{i}
for simplicity and study the univariate function. By orthogonality of the wavelet basis and Parseval’s identity, we can decompose the objective function as

\displaystyle|\!|\!|\widehat{X}-X|\!|\!|^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell=1}^{2^{j_{0}}}\left(\widehat{\xi}_{j_{0}\ell}-{\xi}_{j_{0}\ell}\right)^{2}+\sum_{j=j_{0}}^{J-1}\sum_{\ell=1}^{2^{j}}\left(\widehat{\eta}_{j\ell}-{\eta}_{j\ell}\right)^{2}+\sum_{j=J}^{\infty}\sum_{\ell=1}^{2^{j}}\eta_{j\ell}^{2}

\displaystyle=T_{1}+T_{2}+T_{3},

where
J=\log_{2}N
.
Hence, the goal is to find the upper bounds for
T_{1},T_{2},T_{3}
.
To bound
T_{1},T_{2},T_{3}
, we will introduce additional terms. Recall that we first interpolate the discrete samples with the basis function
\Phi_{Jn}
to construct the continuous function:

\displaystyle\widetilde{X}(t)

\displaystyle=N^{-1/2}\sum_{n=1}^{N}Y_{n}\phi_{Jn}(t).

Now, define that

\displaystyle\Delta(t)

\displaystyle=N^{-1/2}\sum_{n=1}^{N}X(t_{n})\phi_{Jn}(t)-{X}(t);

\displaystyle r(t)

\displaystyle=N^{-1/2}\sigma^{\star}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\varepsilon_{n}\phi_{Jn}(t),

where
\phi_{Jn}=2^{J/2}\phi(2^{J}t-n)
. It follows that

\widetilde{X}(t)=X(t)+\Delta(t)+r(t).

Denote the subspace
V_{J}
to be the closed linear subspace of
\{\phi_{Jn},n=1,\ldots,2^{J}=N\}
. The projection to
V_{J}
of
\widetilde{X}
, denoted as
\widetilde{X}_{J}=P_{V_{J}}\widetilde{X}
, can be decomposed as

\widetilde{X}_{J}(t)=X_{J}(t)+\Delta_{J}(t)+r_{J}(t),

where
X_{J}=P_{V_{J}}X
,
\Delta_{J}=P_{V_{J}}\Delta
, and
r_{J}=P_{V_{J}}r
. Hence, by construction, we can write,

\displaystyle\widehat{\xi}_{j_{0}\ell}

\displaystyle=\langle{X_{J}},{\phi_{j_{0}\ell}}\rangle+\langle{\Delta_{J}},{\phi_{j_{0}\ell}}\rangle+\langle{r_{J}},{\phi_{j_{0}\ell}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\xi_{j_{0}\ell}+d^{\prime}_{j_{0}\ell}+r^{\prime}_{j_{0}\ell},

\displaystyle\ell=1,\ldots,2^{j_{0}}.

Furthermore, for
j=j_{0},\ldots,J-1

\displaystyle\widetilde{\eta}_{j\ell}

\displaystyle=\langle{X_{J}},{\psi_{j\ell}}\rangle+\langle{\Delta_{J}},{\psi_{j\ell}}\rangle+\langle{r_{J}},{\psi_{j\ell}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\eta_{j\ell}+d_{j\ell}+r_{j\ell},

\displaystyle\ell=1,\ldots,2^{j}.

(A.1)

For each
j
, we define the set

\Xi_{j}=\{\ell:\text{supp}(\psi_{j\ell})\text{ contains at least one jump point of $X(t)$}\}.

Therefore, by Lemma
A.1
, we can bound
T_{3}
as

\displaystyle T_{3}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=J}^{\infty}\sum_{\ell\in\Xi_{j}}\eta_{j\ell}^{2}+\sum_{j=J}^{\infty}\sum_{\ell\not\in\Xi_{j}}\eta_{j\ell}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=J}^{\infty}L(d+2)C^{2}2^{-j}+\sum_{j=J}^{\infty}\sum_{\ell\not\in\Xi_{j}}C^{2}2^{-j(1+2\alpha)}

\displaystyle=o\left(N^{-2\alpha/(1+2\alpha)}\right),

where
L
is the length of the support of the mother wavelet and
d
is the number of switchings.

To find the upper bound of
T_{1}
, we can write

T_{1}=\sum_{\ell=1}^{2^{j_{0}}}\left(\widehat{\xi}_{j_{0}\ell}-{\xi}_{j_{0}\ell}\right)^{2}=\sum_{\ell=1}^{2^{j_{0}}}\left(r_{j_{0}\ell}^{\prime}+d_{j_{0}\ell}^{\prime}\right)^{2}\leq 2\sum_{\ell=1}^{2^{j_{0}}}(r_{j_{0}\ell}^{\prime})^{2}+2\sum_{\ell=1}^{2^{j_{0}}}(d_{j_{0}\ell}^{\prime})^{2}.

Since
r_{j_{0}\ell}^{\prime}=N^{-1/2}\sigma^{\star}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\varepsilon_{n}\langle{\phi_{Jn}},{\phi_{j_{0}\ell}}\rangle
, where
\varepsilon_{n}
for
n=1\ldots,N
are
i.i.d.
standard Gaussian and the basis function can be decomposed as
\phi_{j_{0}\ell}=\sum_{n=1}^{N}\langle{\phi_{Jn}},{\phi_{j_{0}\ell}}\rangle\phi_{Jn}
. It follows that
r_{j_{0}\ell}^{\prime}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,N^{-1}(\sigma^{\star})^{2})
. Using the Gaussian tail bound, for each
\ell=1,\ldots,2^{j_{0}}
, we have

P\left(\left|r_{j_{0}\ell}^{\prime}\right|\geq\sigma^{\star}\sqrt{\frac{2\log N/\delta}{N}}\right)\leq\frac{2\delta}{N}.

Taking the union bound across
\ell=1,\ldots,2^{j_{0}}
, we have

P\left(\max_{\ell=1,\ldots,2^{j_{0}}}\left|r_{j_{0}\ell}^{\prime}\right|\geq\sigma^{\star}\sqrt{\frac{2\log N/\delta}{N}}\right)\leq\frac{2^{j_{0}+1}\delta}{N}.

We denote the event
\mathcal{E}_{1}
as the event that
\max_{\ell=1,\ldots,2^{j_{0}}}\left|r_{j_{0}\ell}^{\prime}\right|\leq\sigma^{\star}\sqrt{{2\log(N/\delta)}/{N}}
. Since
2^{j_{0}+1}\leq N
, it follow directly that
\mathcal{E}_{1}
happens with probability at least
1-\delta
. Conditioned on event
\mathcal{E}_{1}
, we have
T_{1}

T_{1}\leq 2^{j_{0}+1}(\sigma^{\star})^{2}\frac{\log N/\delta}{N}+2\sum_{\ell=1}^{2^{j_{0}}}(d^{\prime}_{j_{0}k})^{2}.

By Lemma
A.2
, it follows that

|\!|\!|\Delta_{J}|\!|\!|^{2}=\sum_{\ell=1}^{2^{j_{0}}}(d^{\prime}_{j_{0}\ell})^{2}+\sum_{j=j_{0}+1}^{J-1}\sum_{\ell=1}^{2^{j}}(d_{j\ell})^{2}=o(N^{-2\alpha/(1+2\alpha)}).

(A.2)

Hence,

T_{1}\leq 2^{j_{0}+1}\sigma^{2}\frac{\log N/\delta}{N}+2\sum_{\ell=1}^{2^{j_{0}}}(d^{\prime}_{j_{0}k})^{2}=o\left(\{\log(N/\delta)/N\}^{2\alpha/(1+2\alpha)}\right).

To bound
T_{2}
, we decompose into two terms

\displaystyle T_{2}=\sum_{j=j_{0}}^{J-1}\sum_{\ell\in\Xi_{j}}(\eta_{j\ell}-\widehat{\eta}_{j\ell})^{2}+\sum_{j=j_{0}}^{J-1}\sum_{\ell\not\in\Xi_{j}}(\eta_{j\ell}-\widehat{\eta}_{j\ell})^{2}=T_{21}+T_{22}.

(A.3)

Before proceeding, we consider the event
\mathcal{E}_{2}
be the event that

\max_{j,\ell}\left|r_{j\ell}\right|\leq C\sigma^{\star}\sqrt{\frac{2\log N/\delta}{N}}=\frac{1}{3}\lambda_{j,\ell}.

We can write

\displaystyle\max_{j,\ell}\left|r_{j\ell}\right|

\displaystyle=\max_{j,\ell}\left|N^{-1/2}\sigma^{\star}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\varepsilon_{n}\langle{\phi_{Jn}},{\psi_{j\ell}}\rangle\right|.

By Cauchy–Schwarz inequality, we have

\displaystyle\leq N^{-1/2}\sigma^{\star}\max_{n}|\varepsilon_{n}|\max_{j,\ell}\sum_{n^{\prime}=1}^{N}|\langle{\phi_{Jn^{\prime}}},{\psi_{j\ell}}\rangle|.

(A.4)

Note that
\psi_{j\ell}(t)=2^{j/2}\psi(2^{j/2}t-\ell)
, and hence we can apply Lemma
A.1
and obtain that

\left|\langle{\psi_{j\ell}},{\phi_{Jn}}\rangle\right|\leq C2^{j/2-J(1/2+\alpha)}.

Summing over
n^{\prime}=1,\ldots,2^{J}=N
,
we can write

\displaystyle\sum_{n^{\prime}=1}^{N}\left|\langle{\psi_{j\ell}},{\phi_{Jn^{\prime}}}\rangle\right|\leq C2^{j/2+J/2-J\alpha}.

Since
j\leq J
and
\alpha\geq 1
, then it follows that

\max_{j,\ell}\sum_{n^{\prime}=1}^{N}\left|\langle{\phi_{Jn^{\prime}}},{\psi_{j\ell}}\rangle\right|\leq C,

is bounded by a constant
C
.
Therefore, we can bound (
A.4
) as

\max_{j,\ell}\left|r_{j\ell}\right|\leq CN^{-1/2}\sigma^{\star}\max_{n}\left|\varepsilon_{n}\right|.

Therefore, event
\mathcal{E}_{2}
holds true if

\max_{n}N^{-1/2}\sigma^{\star}\left|\varepsilon_{n}\right|\leq\sigma^{\star}\sqrt{\frac{2\log N/\delta}{N}}.

For each
N^{-1/2}\sigma^{\star}\left|\varepsilon_{n}\right|
, we can apply the Gaussian tail bound and obtain

P\left(N^{-1/2}\sigma^{\star}\left|\varepsilon_{n}\right|\geq\sigma^{\star}\sqrt{\frac{2\log N/\delta}{N}}\right)\leq 2\frac{\delta}{N}.

Taking union bound of
n=1,\ldots,N
, we have

P\left(\max_{n}N^{-1/2}\sigma^{\star}\left|\varepsilon_{n}\right|\geq\sigma^{\star}\sqrt{\frac{2\log N/\delta}{N}}\right)\leq 2\delta.

Therefore, we can conclude that event
\mathcal{E}_{2}
happens with probability at least
1-2\delta
. Since
\alpha>1/2
, by (
A.2
), we have
|d_{j\ell}|\leq\sigma^{\star}(2\log(N/\delta)/N)^{1/2}=3^{-1}\lambda_{j\ell}
. On the event
\mathcal{E}_{2}
, we can apply Lemma
A.3
to (
A.3
) and obtain

\displaystyle T_{21}=\sum_{j=j_{0}}^{J-1}\sum_{\ell\in\Xi_{j}}(\eta_{j\ell}-\widehat{\eta}_{j\ell})^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=j_{0}}^{J-1}L(d+2)\left(\frac{10}{3}\lambda_{j\ell}^{2}+3d_{j\ell}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{11}{3}\sum_{j=j_{0}}^{J-1}L(d+2)\lambda_{j\ell}^{2}=o\left((\log(N/\delta)/N)^{2\alpha/(1+2\alpha)}\right),

where
\lambda_{j\ell}=3\sigma^{\star}\sqrt{(2\log N/\delta)/N}
.

Finally, to find the upper bound of
T_{22}
, we consider further splitting it into two terms:

T_{22}=\sum_{j=j_{0}}^{J_{1}-1}\sum_{\ell\not\in\Xi_{j}}(\eta_{j\ell}-\widehat{\eta}_{j\ell})^{2}+\sum_{j=J_{1}}^{J-1}\sum_{\ell\not\in\Xi_{j}}(\eta_{j\ell}-\widehat{\eta}_{j\ell})^{2},

where

J_{1}=C^{\prime}\left\lfloor\frac{1}{1+2\alpha}\log_{2}\frac{N}{2(\sigma^{\star})^{2}\log(N/\delta)}\right\rfloor,

where
C^{\prime}
is an absolute constant.
Note that, conditioned on event
\mathcal{E}_{2}
, for the first term of
T_{22}
, we can apply Lemma
A.3
again and obtain

\sum_{j=j_{0}}^{J_{1}-1}\sum_{\ell\not\in\Xi_{j}}(\eta_{j\ell}-\widehat{\eta}_{j\ell})^{2}\leq\sum_{j=j_{0}}^{J_{1}-1}2^{j}\left(\frac{10}{3}\lambda_{j\ell}^{2}+d_{j\ell}^{2}\right)=O\left((\log(N/\delta)/N)^{2\alpha/(1+2\alpha)}\right).

Finally, for
j\geq J_{1}
and
\ell\not\in\Xi_{j}
, apply Lemma
A.1
, we have

\left|\eta_{j\ell}\right|\leq\sigma^{\star}\sqrt{\frac{2\log N/\delta}{N}}.

Conditioned on the event
\mathcal{E}_{2}
and
d_{j\ell}\leq\sigma^{\star}(\frac{2\log N/\delta}{N})^{1/2}
, we can conclude that

\displaystyle\left|\widetilde{\eta}_{j\ell}\right|\leq 3\sigma^{\star}\sqrt{\frac{2\log N/\delta}{N}}=\lambda_{j\ell}.

(A.5)

Since
\widehat{\eta}_{j\ell}=sgn(\widetilde{\eta}_{j\ell})(|\widetilde{\eta}_{j\ell}|-\lambda_{j\ell})_{+}
, we can conclude that

\left(\widehat{\eta}_{j\ell}-\eta_{j\ell}\right)^{2}=\eta_{j\ell}^{2},

for
j\geq J_{1}
and
\ell\not\in\Xi_{j}
. Therefore, we have

\sum_{j=J_{1}}^{J-1}\sum_{\ell\not\in\Xi_{j}}(\eta_{j\ell}-\widehat{\eta}_{j\ell})^{2}=\sum_{j=J_{1}}^{J-1}\sum_{\ell\not\in\Xi_{j}}\eta_{j\ell}^{2}\leq C\sum_{j=J_{1}}^{J-1}2^{j}2^{-j(1+2\alpha)}=O(N^{-2\alpha}).

(A.6)

Combining results of (
A.5
)–(
A.6
), we have
T_{2}=O(\{\log(N/\delta)/N\}^{2\alpha/(1+2\alpha)})
.
Conditioned on events
\mathcal{E}_{1},\mathcal{E}_{2}
, we can conclude that

|\!|\!|\widehat{X}-X|\!|\!|^{2}=T_{1}+T_{2}=T_{3}=C\left(\frac{\log N-\log\delta}{N}\right)^{2\alpha/(1+2\alpha)}.

Furthermore,
P(\mathcal{E}_{1}\cap\mathcal{E}_{2})\geq 1-3\delta
.
∎

Lemma A.1

(Lemma 1 in
Brown and Cai (1998)
)
.

Let
f\in\Lambda^{\alpha}(M,B,d)
. Suppose that the wavelet function
\psi
is
r
-regular with
r\geq\alpha
. Then:

1.

If supp
(\psi_{j\ell})
does not contain any jump points of
f
, then

\eta_{j\ell}=\left|\langle{f},{\psi_{j\ell}}\rangle\right|\leq C2^{-j(1/2+\alpha)}.

2.

If supp
(\psi_{j\ell})
contains jump points of
f
, then

\eta_{j\ell}=\left|\langle{f},{\psi_{j\ell}}\rangle\right|\leq C2^{-j/2}.

Lemma A.2

(Adapted from Theorem 1 in
Brown and Cai (1998)
)
.

Suppose that an uniformly sampled function
\{f(t_{n}),n=1,\ldots,N\}
is given with
t_{n}=n/N
for
n=1,\ldots,N
. Let the wavelet function
\psi
be
r
-regular with
r\geq\alpha
. Define
\widehat{f}(t)=N^{-1/2}\sum_{n=1}^{N}f(t_{n})\phi_{Jn}(t)
. Then, the approximation error satisfies

\sup_{f\in\Lambda^{\alpha}(M,B,d)}|\!|\!|\widehat{f}-f|\!|\!|^{2}=o(N^{-2\alpha/(1+2\alpha)}).

Proof of Lemma
A.2
.

The proof is a special case of Theorem 1 in
Brown and Cai (1998)
, where the cumulative density function
H
we use here is an identify function.
∎

Lemma A.3
.

Recall the definitions of
r_{j\ell}
and
d_{j\ell}
in (
A.1
).
Suppose that
|r_{j\ell}|\leq 3^{-1}\lambda_{j\ell}
and
|d_{j\ell}|\leq 3^{-1}\lambda_{j\ell}
, then

\left(\widehat{\eta}_{j\ell}-\eta_{j\ell}\right)^{2}\leq\frac{10}{3}\lambda_{j\ell}^{2}+3d_{j\ell}^{2}.

Proof of Lemma
A.3
.

Recall the soft thresholding estimator defined (
3.1
):

\widehat{\eta}_{i,j\ell}=sgn(\widetilde{\eta}_{i,j\ell})(|\widetilde{\eta}_{i,j\ell}|-\lambda_{j\ell})_{+}.

We consider three cases.

Case 1
. Suppose that
\left|\eta_{j\ell}\right|<3^{-1}\lambda_{j\ell}
, we have

\left|\widetilde{\eta}_{j\ell}\right|=\left|\eta_{j\ell}+r_{j\ell}+d_{j\ell}\right|\leq\left|\eta_{j\ell}\right|+\left|r_{j\ell}\right|+\left|d_{j\ell}\right|\leq\lambda_{j\ell}.

Hence

\left(\widehat{\eta}_{j\ell}-\eta_{j\ell}\right)^{2}=\eta_{j\ell}^{2}\leq\frac{1}{9}\lambda_{j\ell}^{2}.

Case 2
. Suppose that
\left|\eta_{j\ell}\right|>(5/3)\lambda_{j\ell}
. In this case, we have

\left|\widetilde{\eta}_{j\ell}\right|\geq\left|\eta_{j\ell}\right|-\left|r_{j\ell}\right|-\left|d_{j\ell}\right|\geq\lambda_{j\ell}.

Hence

\left(\widehat{\eta}_{j\ell}-\eta_{j\ell}\right)^{2}=(d_{j\ell}+r_{j\ell}-\text{sgn}(\widetilde{\eta}_{j\ell})\lambda_{j\ell})^{2}\leq 3d_{j\ell}^{2}+\frac{10}{3}\lambda_{j\ell}^{2}.

Case 3
. Suppose that
(1/3)\lambda_{j\ell}\leq\left|\eta_{j\ell}\right|\leq(5/3)\lambda_{j\ell}
. In this case,

\left(\widehat{\eta}_{j\ell}-\eta_{j\ell}\right)^{2}\leq(3d_{j\ell}^{2}+\frac{10}{3}\lambda_{j\ell}^{2})\vee\frac{1}{9}\lambda_{j\ell}^{2}\leq 3d_{j\ell}^{2}+\frac{10}{3}\lambda_{j\ell}^{2}.

Hence, we can conclude the results.
∎

Appendix B
The
\beta
-mixing Markov processes

This manuscript focuses on the analysis of of
\beta
-mixing Markov processes. Hence, it is important to understand the sufficient conditions for a Markov process to be strictly stationary and
\beta
-mixing. It is known that the positive recurrent process has a unique stationary distribution
(
Yin and Zhu, 2010
, Theorem 4.3)
. The path to check whether a process is
\beta
-mixing, is often not straightforward and consists of several steps.
Our method is built on the integration of several pieces of foundational studies
(
Meyn and Tweedie, 1992
;
Meyn and Tweedie, 1993a
;
Meyn and Tweedie, 1993b
;
Meyn and Tweedie, 2012
)
. Instead of directly verifying the
\beta
-mixing property, we start with checking the ergodicity of a process. The ergodicity describes a process converging to a unique stationary distribution in the total variation distance. In the case of the Markov process, this property is closely connected to the
\beta
-mixing property, which characterizes the (in)dependency of two time points separated by an infinite number of time points. This is because once the process enters the stationary state, the initial condition does not matter to the state of the current process and hence the independence of two time points is granted.
Davydov (1974)
first formalized the relationship between mixing and ergodicity coefficients.
As a result, our proof steps start with verifying the Foster-Lyapunov inequality for the generator of the process, which leads to the verification of the geometric ergodicity property
(
Meyn and Tweedie, 1993a
)
. Finally, with geometric ergodicity and other conditions, one can verify the
\beta
-mixing property.
To begin with, we review useful tools for the theories. Section
B.1
introduces the generator of a Markov process; Section
B.2
discusses the details of mixing and ergodic process.

B.1
Markov processes and their generators

In this section, we will review the basic properties of Markov processes with a focus on the construction of a Markov process.
First, we introduce the definition of the Markov Process.

Definition 5

(Markov Process)
.

Let
\mathscr{B}
be the
\sigma
-field Borel sets in
\mathbb{R}^{p}
.
A stochastic process
X(t)\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
, defined for
t\geq 0
on the probability space
(\Omega,\mathscr{B},P)
is a Markov process, if for all
A\in\mathscr{B}
and
0\leq s<t
,

P(X(t)\in A\mid\sigma(\{X(u),u\leq s\}))=P(X(t)\in A\mid X(s)),

where
\sigma(\{X(u),u\leq s\})
is the
\sigma
-field generated by
\{X(u),u\leq s\}
.

Hence, the ODE process:

\mathrm{d}x=f(x_{t})\mathrm{d}t,

(B.1)

can be shown as a Markov process
(
Khasminskii, 2011
)
. Furthermore, it is easy to see that the joint processes (
2.1
)–(
2.2
) are Markov processes
(
Yin and Zhu, 2010
)
.

Now let us stick with the simpler Markov process of the form (
B.1
). From Definition
5
, we can define a transition probability function as

P(X(t)\in A\mid X(s)=x)=p(s,x,t,A),

which satisfies the Chapman-Kolmogorov equation:

p(s,x,t,A)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{p}}p(s,x,u,\mathrm{d}y)p(u,y,t,A),\quad s<u<t.

(B.2)

With the transition probability function, we can construct the Markov process with any initial distribution. A time-homogeneous Markov process is a process with the transition function independent of s:
p(s,x,t,A)=p(s+u,x,t+u,A)
for any
u>0
. Hence, we can write
p(s,x,t,A)=p(x,t-s,A)
.

Now, suppose that
(X(t))_{t\geq 0}
is a homogeneous Markov process with transition probability function
p(x,t,A)
. Then we define the operator
T_{t}
:

\displaystyle T_{t}V(x)=\int p(x,t,dy)V(y)=\mathbb{E}^{x}[V(X(t))].

(B.3)

Furthermore, by (
B.2
), we see that
T_{t+s}=T_{t}T_{s}
and hence
T_{t}
is a homogeneous semigroup.
Then the generator is defined as

\mathscr{L}V(x)=\lim_{t\rightarrow+0}\frac{T_{t}V(x)-V(x)}{t}.

With the generator, one can uniquely define the continuous transition probability function
(
Khasminskii, 2011
, Chapter 3)
.

Generator of the Markov-switching ODE process.

Now, let us work on the switching ODE process discussed in this manuscript:

\mathrm{d}x=f(x_{t},z_{t})dt,

where
z_{t}\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
for
t\geq 0
takes on a discrete value. Essentially
(Z(t))_{t\geq 0}
is a continuous-time Markov chain with generator matrix
Q
. The underlying transition function
P(X(t)\in A,Z(t)\in B\mid X(0)=x,Z(0)=z)=p((x,z),t,(A,B))
is time-homogeneous. Then the generator is defined similarly as:

\mathscr{L}V(x,z)=\lim_{t\rightarrow+0}\frac{T_{t}V(x,z)-V(x,z)}{t},

with
T_{t}V(x,z)=\int p((x,z),t,(\mathrm{dy},\mathrm{dw}))V(y,w)
.

If
V(\cdot,\ell)
for each
\ell
is a sufficiently smooth function, then the generator operator of such process is defined as the following
(
Yin and Zhu, 2010
, Chapter 2)
:

\mathscr{L}V(x,\ell)=\mathscr{A}V(\cdot,\ell)(x)+\mathscr{Q}V(x,\cdot)(\ell),

(B.4)

with

\mathscr{A}V(\cdot,\ell)(x)=\nabla_{x}V(\cdot,\ell)^{\top}f(x,\ell),\quad\mathscr{Q}V(x,\cdot)(\ell)=\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}q_{\ell\ell^{\prime}}V(x,\ell^{\prime}).

With the generator, we can also determine the stability, ergodicity, and mixing properties of the Markov process. We selectively review the results that are most pertinent to our analysis. We refer
Khasminskii (2011)
;
Meyn and Tweedie (2012)
for more comprehensive discussions.

B.2
Mixing and ergodicity of Markov processes

This section introduces the connection of the mixing property and the ergodicity property of a Markov process.
First, we define the
\beta
-mixing property. From a high-level perspective, the mixing property describes the dependency of a stochastic process: if we take any two random variables
X(s)
,
X(t)
from a stochastic process, they will become asymptotically independent as the time difference
|t-s|
goes to infinity. Definition
1
defines the
\beta
-mixing for discrete stochastic processes, here we define similarly for continuous-time stochastic processes.

Definition 6

(
\beta
-mixing for continuous-time process)
.

Given
\ell\geq 0
, the
\beta
-mixing coefficient is defined as,

\beta(\ell)=\sup_{t}\beta(\mathcal{F}_{-\infty}^{t},\mathcal{F}^{\infty}_{t+\ell})=\sup_{t}\|P_{t,\ell}-P_{-\infty}^{t}\otimes P_{t+\ell}^{\infty}\|_{\text{TV}},

where
\mathcal{F}_{-\infty}^{t}=\sigma(\{X(u):u\in(\infty,t]\})
,
\mathcal{F}_{t+\ell}^{\infty}=\sigma(\{X(u):u\in[t+\ell,\infty)\})
.

P_{t,\ell}
associates with the
\sigma
-field
(\mathcal{F}_{-\infty}^{t}\vee\mathcal{F}_{t+\ell}^{\infty})
,
P_{-\infty}^{t}
associates with the
\sigma
-field
\mathcal{F}_{-\infty}^{t}
, and
P_{t+\ell}^{\infty}
associates with the
\sigma
-field
\mathcal{F}_{t+\ell}^{\infty}
. A stochastic
process is said to be absolutely regular, or
\beta
-mixing,
if
\beta(\ell)\rightarrow 0
as
\ell\rightarrow\infty
.

Hence, we say that a stochastic process is geometric
\beta
-mixing if
\beta(\ell)\leq exp(-c\ell)
for some positive constant
c
.

Oftentimes, given a stochastic process, it is hard to verify the
\beta
-mixing property. As an alternative, we can first verify whether the process is ergodic or not, which can be checked using Foster-Lyapunov criterion. Let us define the ergodicity of a Markov process in the following.

Definition 7
.

A Markov process is called ergodic if a stationary distribution
\pi
exists and

\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}\|p(x,t,\cdot)-\pi\|_{\text{TV}}=0,\quad x\in\mathcal{X}.

It is known that if a process is positive recurrent and if any discrete-sampled chain is irreducible, then the process ergodic
(
Meyn and Tweedie, 1992
, Theorem 6.1)
.
As mentioned earlier, if a Markov process is ergodic, then it is also mixing under additional mild conditions. The following lemma formalizes the relation between ergodicity and
\beta
-mixing
(
Davydov, 1974
;
Masuda, 2007
)
.

Lemma B.1

(Lemma 3.9 in
Masuda (2007)
)
.

Let
(X(t))_{t\geq 0}
be a Markov process. Let
\eta
,
(p(\cdot,t,\cdot))_{t\geq 0}
and
\beta_{X}(t)
respectively denote initial distribution, transition function, and
\beta
-mixing coefficient of
(X(t))_{t\geq 0}
. Suppose that there exist probability measure
\pi
on
(\mathcal{X},\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{X}))
, measurable function
B
, and deterministic sequence
(\delta(t))_{t\geq 0}
tending to
0
as
t\rightarrow\infty
for which

1.

\|p(x,t,\cdot)-\pi\|_{\text{TV}}\leq B(x)\delta(t)
for which
t\geq 0
and
x\in\mathcal{X}
;

2.

\kappa:=\sup_{s\geq 0}\int B(x)\eta p(\cdot,s,dx)<\infty
.

Then
\beta_{X}(t)\leq 2\kappa\delta(t)
for any
t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
, that is,
X
is
\beta
-mixing at rate
\delta(t)
.

Hence, to show that a process is geometric
\beta
-mixing, it suffices to show that a process is geometric ergodicity defined in the following.

Definition 8

(Geometric Ergodicity)
.

Suppose that the diffusion process
(X(t))_{t\geq 0}
is positive recurrent and it has an unique stationary distribution
\pi
. We say that
X(t)
is geometrically ergodic if there exists a constant
\gamma>0
and a real valued function
B
such that for all
t>0
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
:

\|p(x,t,\cdot)-\pi\|_{\text{TV}}\leq B(x)\exp(-\gamma t).

B.2.1
Foster-Lyapunov criterion

Now, we have shown the connection between ergodicity and mixing. The next step is to understand what characterizes a stochastic process to be geometric ergodic.

Meyn and Tweedie (1993a)
showed that one can apply the Foster-Lyapunov criterion to check the geometric ergodicity of a stochastic process. We first introduce the criterion. Recall in Section
B.1
that
\mathscr{L}
is a generator of a Markov process
(X(t))_{t\geq 0}
.

Assumption 11
.

There exists a function
V\in\text{Dom}(\mathscr{L})
and
V(x)\rightarrow\infty
as
x\rightarrow\infty
, and for some
c>0
,
d<\infty
such that

\mathscr{L}V(x)\leq-cV(x)+d.

Assumption
11
is a special case of (CD3) in
Meyn and Tweedie (1993a)
. (CD3) is defined on the extended generator
\mathscr{L}_{m}
on the stopped process (see
Meyn and Tweedie (1993a)
for the definition) whereas Assumption
11
is defined on the generator of the process
\mathscr{L}
. However, under the condition that
V\in\text{Dom}(\mathscr{L})
, we have
\mathscr{L}V=\mathscr{L}_{m}V
. Having Assumption
11
alone is not sufficient to show the geometric ergodicity. Instead, we need to ensure that there exists a discrete-sampled chain of the original continuous process that behaves nicely on every compact set of
\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{X})
, known as the petite set
(
Meyn and Tweedie, 1992
)
. Before stating the theorem, we introduce three additional terms.

Definition 9

(Skeleton)
.

The
\widetilde{h}
-skeleton chain of
(X(t))_{t\geq 0}
is
X_{n}^{(\widetilde{h})}=X(n\widetilde{h})
for a constant
\widetilde{h}>0
and
n\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
.

Hence, by definition,
\{X_{n}^{(\widetilde{h})}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a discrete-time Markov chain. Now, for simplicity of notation, suppose that a discrete-time Markov chain
\{X_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
taking values in
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{X})
be the Borel sets of
\mathcal{X}
. We define the one-step transition probability function as
p(x,A):=p(x,h,A)
for
x\in\mathcal{X}
and
A\in\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{X})
. We define a distribution
a=\{a(n)\}
on
{n\in\mathbb{N}}
as the sampling distribution that samples the time points of
\{X_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
. We call this sampled chain as
X^{a}_{n}
associated with the transition kernel function:

K_{a}(x,A)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}p^{n}(x,A)a(n).

(B.5)

Definition 10

(Petite Set, adapted from Section 5.5.2 in
Meyn and Tweedie (2012)
)
.

A set
C\in\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{X})
is
\nu_{a}
petite if the transition kernel function of the sampled chain satisfies:

K_{a}(x,B)\geq\nu_{a}(B),

for all
x\in C
and
B\in\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{X})
, where
\nu_{a}
is a non-trivial measure on
\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{X})
.

Finally, we define the
f
-norm in the following.

Definition 11

(
f
-norm)
.

For any positive measurable function
f\geq 1
and any signed measure:

\|\mu\|_{f}=\sup_{\left|g\right|\leq f}\left|\mu(g)\right|.

Under this definition, the total variation norm is equivalent as the
f
-norm with constant function
f=1
. With the definitions of skeleton, petite set, and
f
-norm, we introduce the theory.

Lemma B.2

(Theorem 6.1 of
Meyn and Tweedie (1993a)
)
.

Suppose that
(X(t))_{t\geq 0}
is a right process, and that all compact sets are petite for some skeleton chain. If Assumption
11
holds, then there exists
\gamma>0
and
B<\infty
such that

\|p(x,t,\cdot)-\pi\|_{f}\leq Bf(x)\exp(-\gamma t),\quad t\geq 0,x\in\mathcal{X},

(B.6)

with
f=V+1
, where
V
is defined in Assumption
11
.

It is easy to see that
\|p(x,t,\cdot)-\pi\|_{\text{TV}}\leq\|p(x,t,\cdot)-\pi\|_{V+1}
and hence if (
B.6
) holds true, then the process is geometric ergodic.
Hence, to check the geometric ergodicity of a stochastic process, one can verify whether Assumption
11
holds true. Additionally, to verify that the conditions all compact sets are petite for a skeleton chain, we can apply Theorem 3.4 in
(
Meyn and Tweedie, 1992
)
. We review them in the next section.

B.2.2
Petite set and skeleton chain

This section introduces the conditions when all compact sets are petite, required by Lemma
B.2
. As Lemma
B.3
indicates, there is a close connection between the Feller property and the irreducibility of a Markov chain to the petite sets. Let
p(\cdot,\cdot)
be the transition kernel of a discrete chain. For example, the transition kernel of a
h
-skeleton chain is defined as
p(x,A)=p(x,h,A)
for
x\in\mathcal{X}
and
\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{X})
. Define the quantity

G(x,A)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}p^{n}(x,A).

If
G(x,A)>0
, it implies that starting from
x
,
A
is reachable with positive probability. We introduce the following definition from
(
Meyn and Tweedie, 1992
)
.

Definition 12

(
\varphi
-irreducibility)
.

\{X_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is
\varphi
-irreducible if there exists a finite measure
\varphi
such that
G(x,A)>0
for all
x\in\mathcal{X}
whenever
\varphi(A)>0
.
\varphi
is called a irreducible measure.

The Feller property characterizes the continuity of the transition kernel.

Definition 13
.

If the transition kernel
p(\cdot,\cdot)
maps bounded continuous functions to bounded continuous functions, then it is weak Feller. If the transition kernel
p(\cdot,\cdot)
maps all bounded measurable functions to bounded continuous functions, then it is strong Feller.

Lemma B.3

(Theorem 3.4 in
(
Meyn and Tweedie, 1992
)
)
.

Suppose that a Markov chain
\{X_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
taking values in
\mathcal{X}
is
\varphi
-irreducible. Then either of the conditions implies that all compact subsets of
\mathcal{X}
are petite:

1.

\{X_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is Feller and an open
\varphi
-positive petite set exists

2.

\{X_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is Feller and
\text{supp}(\varphi)
has non-empty interior.

Hence if either the conditions in Lemma
B.3
holds, we can fulfill partial requirements of Lemma
B.2
.
The Feller property of a stochastic process is fairly straightforward to check. In contrast, to check the
\varphi
-irreducibility required by Lemma
B.3
, one way is to verify whether the Markov chain is a
T
-chain and there exists a reachable point
x^{*}\in\mathcal{X}
. To make our statement more concrete, we define the T-chain and reachable point using the definitions in Section 6 of
Meyn and Tweedie (2012)
.

Definition 14

(T-chain)
.

T is a
continuous component
of
K_{a}
defined in (
B.5
) if

K_{a}(x,A)\geq T(x,A),\quad x\in\mathcal{X},\;A\in\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{X}),

where
T(\cdot,A)
is a lower semicontinuous function for any
A\in\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{X})
.
If
T(x,\mathcal{X})>0
for all
x
, then
\{X_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a T-chain.

Therefore, to verify a that the kernel
T(\cdot,A)
is lower semicontinuous for every
A\in\mathscr{B}
, one can check the following two properties.

Lemma B.4

(Lemma 3.1 in
Cline and Huay-min (1998)
)
.

Assume
\mathcal{X}
is locally compact and
T:\mathcal{X}\times\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{X})\rightarrow[0,1]
is a kernel and
\mu
is a bounded measure on compact sets of
\mathcal{X}
. If

1.

for each
\varepsilon
and compact set
K_{1},K_{2}
, there exists a
\delta>0
such that if
A\subset K_{2}
and
\mu(A)<\delta
, then
\sup_{y\in K_{1}}T(y,A)<\varepsilon
.

2.

T(\cdot,O)
is lower semicontinuous for all (relatively compact) open sets,

then
T(\cdot,A)
is lower semicontinuous for all
A\in\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{X})
.

Definition 15

(Reachable point)
.

A point
x^{*}\in\mathcal{X}
is reachable if for every open neighborhood of
x^{*}
, denoted as
O\in\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{X})
,

\sum_{n}p^{n}(x,O)>0,\quad x\in\mathcal{X}.

Lemma B.5

(Proposition 6.2.1 in
(
Meyn and Tweedie, 2012
)
)
.

If
\{X_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a T-chain, and
\mathcal{X}
contains one reachable point
x^{*}
, then
X_{n}
is
\varphi
-irreducible with
\varphi=T(x^{*},\cdot)
.

In conclusion, we summarize the steps to check the
\beta
-mixing property of a Markov process
(X(t))_{t\geq 0}
. By Lemma
B.1
,
geometrically ergodicity implies geometric
\beta
-mixing. To show that the process is geometrically ergodic, we can apply Lemma
B.2
, which subsequently leads to verifying
Assumption
11
and Lemma
B.3
. We can verify Lemma
B.3
by subsequently verifying the conditions required by Lemma
B.5
. In the next section, we use this workflow to construct a few examples of the switching ODEs that are geometric
\beta
-mixing.

B.2.3
Mixing property of the observed process

In the previous two sections, we introduce the tools to show that the joint process
(Z(t),X(t))
is mixing. If
(Z(t),X(t))
is mixing, then it is more straightforward to show that the observation
Y_{n}
is mixing. To see why, we introduce the following properties.

Lemma B.6

(Lemma 3.6 in
Vidyasagar (2013)
)
.

Suppose a real-valued stochastic process
(X(t))_{t\geq 0}
is
\alpha
-,
\beta
-, or
\phi
-mixing, and that
Y(t)=f(X(t))
where
f:X\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
. Then
(Y(t))_{t\geq 0}
is also
\alpha
-,
\beta
-, or
\phi
-mixing, as appropriate.

Lemma B.7

(Lemma 3.7 in
Vidyasagar (2013)
)
.

Suppose
X_{n}
is
\beta
-mixing, and that
\{U_{n}\}
is i.i.d. and also independent of
\{X_{n}\}
. Suppose
Y_{n}=f(X_{n},U_{n})
, where
f
is a fixed measurable function. Then
\{Y_{n}\}
is also
\beta
-mixing.

B.3
Switching-diffusion processes

In previous sections, we have discussed the tools to check the mixing properties of a stochastic process. In this section, we apply these tools to check the Foster-Lyapunov condition.
We provide two examples such that there exist some functions
V
satisfying
\mathcal{L}V\leq-cV+d
.

B.3.1
Linear model

We first consider a linear model. Let
A_{\ell}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times p}
for
\ell=1,\ldots,k
. We define

\dot{X}(t)=A_{Z(t)}X(t)dt.

We make the following assumption.

Assumption 12
.

Z(t)
has a unique stationary distribution
\pi=(\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{k})
. Let
G
be positive definite matrix and define
\mu_{\ell}=2^{-1}\lambda_{max}(GA_{\ell}G^{-1}+G^{-1}A_{\ell}^{\top}G)
.
There exists a positive definite matrix
G
such that

\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}\pi_{\ell}\mu_{\ell}<0

As we will see soon, Assumption
12
is a sufficient condition for
\mathscr{L}V(x)\leq-bV(x)+c
for some
V
. Assumption
12
required for the switching system is weaker than the single dynamical system. It says, the weighted average of the maximum eigenvalue, where the weight is the stationary distribution, should be negative. This implies that some systems associated with the state
\ell
can be unstable, namely the maximum eigenvalue is positive.

Lemma B.8
.

Let
A_{\ell}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times p}
for
\ell=1,\ldots,k
. We define

\dot{X}(t)=A_{Z(t)}X(t)dt.

Suppose that Assumption
12
holds and
x=0
is the equilibrium point and
\mathcal{X}
is compact. Let
\mathcal{C}^{1}
be the set of continuously differentiable function. Then there exists a
V(x,\ell)
such that
V(\cdot,\ell)\in\mathcal{C}^{1}
for each
\ell
, constants
c_{1},d>0
and

\mathscr{L}V(x,\ell)\leq-c_{1}V(x,\ell)+d,\quad x\in\mathcal{X}.

Proof of Lemma
B.8
.

Our proof closely follows the proof of Theorem 8.8 in
Yin and Zhu (2010)
, where it studies the stability of the switching ODE process. Define
\mu=(\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{k})
,
\upsilon=-\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}\pi_{\ell}\mu_{\ell}
. Let
c=(c_{1},\ldots,c_{k})
be the solution to
Qc=\mu+\upsilon{\bf 1}
.
First, we consider the Lyapunov function

V(x,\ell)=(1-\gamma c_{\ell})(x^{\top}G^{2}x)^{\gamma/2},\quad\ell=1,\ldots,k,

where
\gamma\in(0,1)
and
1-\gamma c_{\ell}>0
for
\ell=1,\ldots,k
. Then it follows that

\nabla_{x}V(x,\ell)=(1-\gamma c_{\ell})(x^{\top}G^{2}x)^{\gamma/2-1}\gamma G^{2}x.

Hence, following (
B.4
) and
q_{\ell\ell}=-\sum_{\ell^{\prime}\neq\ell}q_{\ell\ell^{\prime}}
, for
x\neq 0
, we have

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}V(x,\ell)

\displaystyle=(1-\gamma c_{\ell})\gamma(x^{\top}G^{2}x)^{\gamma/2-1}x^{\top}G^{2}(A_{\ell}x)-\sum_{\ell\neq\ell^{\prime}}q_{\ell\ell^{\prime}}(x^{\top}G^{2}x)^{\gamma/2}\gamma(c_{\ell^{\prime}}-c_{\ell})

\displaystyle=(1-\gamma c_{\ell})\gamma(x^{\top}G^{2}x)^{\gamma/2}\left\{\frac{x^{\top}GA_{\ell}x}{x^{\top}G^{2}x}-\sum_{{\ell^{\prime}}\neq\ell}q_{\ell\ell^{\prime}}\frac{c_{\ell^{\prime}}-c_{\ell}}{1-\gamma c_{\ell}}\right\}.

Note that we can write

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell^{\prime}\neq\ell}q_{\ell\ell^{\prime}}\frac{c_{\ell^{\prime}}-c_{\ell}}{1-\gamma c_{\ell}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell^{\prime}=1}^{k}q_{\ell\ell^{\prime}}c_{\ell^{\prime}}-\sum_{\ell^{\prime}\neq\ell}q_{\ell\ell^{\prime}}\frac{c_{\ell}(1-c_{\ell^{\prime}})}{1-\gamma c_{\ell}}\gamma

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell^{\prime}=1}^{k}q_{\ell\ell^{\prime}}c_{\ell^{\prime}}+O(\gamma),

where
O(\gamma)\rightarrow 0
as
\gamma\rightarrow 0
.

Let
y=Gx
and write

\displaystyle\frac{x^{\top}G^{2}A_{\ell}x}{x^{\top}G^{2}x}

\displaystyle=\frac{x^{\top}(G^{2}A_{\ell}+A_{\ell}^{\top}G^{2})x}{2x^{\top}G^{2}x}

\displaystyle=\frac{y^{\top}G^{-1}(G^{2}A_{\ell}+A_{\ell}^{\top}G^{2})G^{-1}y}{2y^{\top}y}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{\max}(G^{-1}(G^{2}A_{\ell}+A_{\ell}^{\top}G^{2})G^{-1})=\mu_{\ell}.

This yields that

\mathscr{L}V(x,\ell)\leq\gamma V(x,\ell)\left\{\mu_{\ell}-\sum_{\ell^{\prime}=1}^{k}q_{\ell\ell^{\prime}}c_{\ell^{\prime}}+O(\gamma)\right\}.

Since
Qc=\mu+\upsilon{\bf 1}
, it follows that
\mu_{\ell}-\sum_{\ell^{\prime}=1}^{k}q_{\ell\ell^{\prime}}c_{\ell^{\prime}}=-\upsilon<0
. Hence

\mathscr{L}V(x,\ell)\leq-(\upsilon\gamma)V(x,\ell)+\gamma V(x,\ell)O(\gamma)

For a fixed
\gamma
, we then define
c_{1}=\upsilon\gamma>0
.
Since
\mathcal{X}
is bounded and one can choose a proper
\gamma
such that there exists a constant
d>0
such that
\sup_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\gamma V(x,\ell)O(\gamma)<d
.
∎

B.3.2
General setting

We now consider a more general setting. The following two assumptions are common in studying the dynamical systems
(
Skorokhod, 2009
;
Yin and Zhu, 2010
)
.

Assumption 13
.

If
x=0
, then
g(x)=0
. Additionally,
g
is locally Lipschitz.

Assumption 14
.

There exists a constant,
K_{0}>0
such that for each state
\ell=1,\ldots,k
,

\displaystyle\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{p}\theta_{i}^{\ell}g(x_{i})\right\|_{2}\leq K_{0}(1+\|x\|_{2}),

where
\theta_{i}^{\ell}=[\theta_{ji}^{\ell}]\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times m}

Assumption
13
–
14
guarantee the uniqueness of the solution
(
Skorokhod, 2009
;
Yin and Zhu, 2010
)
.

Lemma B.9
.

Suppose that Assumption
13
–
14
hold. Given
\ell=1,\ldots,k
, assume that for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
such that

x^{\top}\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{p}\theta_{i}^{\ell\star}g(x_{i})\right\}\leq\beta_{\ell}\|x\|_{2}^{2}+\alpha,

for some constants
\beta_{\ell},\alpha
. If

A=-2\text{diag}(\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{k})-Q^{\star},

is an nonsingular M-matrix. Then, there exists a
V(x,\ell)
such that
V(\cdot,\ell)\in\mathcal{C}^{1}
for each
\ell
, constants
c_{1},d>0
and

\mathscr{L}V(x,\ell)\leq-c_{1}V(x,\ell)+d,\quad x\in\mathcal{X}.

Proof.

This analysis is inspired by Theorem 5.1 in
Yuan and Mao (2003)
, where it discusses the stability of the Markov-switching SDEs. Here, we use their idea to construct the Lyapunov functions. By the property of nonsingular M-matrix, there exists a positive vector, where all the entries are positive values,
\upsilon=(\upsilon_{1},\ldots,\upsilon_{k})^{\top}
such that

\widetilde{c}=A\upsilon,

and entries of
\widetilde{c}
are all positive. Define the function

V(x,\ell)=\upsilon_{\ell}\|x\|_{2}^{2}.

Then, we can write

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}V(x,\ell)

\displaystyle=2\upsilon_{\ell}x^{\top}\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{p}\theta_{i}^{\ell\star}g(x_{i})\right\}+\sum_{\ell^{\prime}=1}^{k}q_{\ell\ell^{\prime}}^{\star}\upsilon_{\ell}\|x\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left(2\upsilon_{\ell}\beta_{\ell}+\sum_{\ell^{\prime}=1}^{k}q_{\ell\ell^{\prime}}^{\star}\upsilon_{\ell}\right)\|x\|_{2}^{2}+2\alpha\upsilon_{\ell}

\displaystyle=-\widetilde{c}_{\ell}\|x\|_{2}^{2}+2\alpha\upsilon_{\ell}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\widetilde{c}_{\ell}}{\upsilon_{\ell}}\upsilon_{\ell}\|x\|_{2}^{2}+2\alpha\upsilon_{\ell}.

Define
c_{1}=\min\widetilde{c}_{\ell}\upsilon_{\ell}^{-1}
and
d=\max_{\ell}2\alpha\upsilon_{\ell}
, then the above term can be bounded as

\displaystyle\leq-c_{1}V(x,\ell)+d.

Then, we complete the proof.
∎

B.4
Irreducible chain

In this section, we discuss the properties that the joint stochastic process
W(t)=(Z(t),X(t))
for
t\geq
is irreducible. We make the following assumptions.

Assumption 15
.

For every
(x,\ell)
with
x\in\mathcal{X}
and
\ell=1,\ldots,k
, we have
p((x^{\prime},\ell^{\prime}),h,(O,\ell))>0
for every open neighbor
O\in\mathscr{B}({\mathcal{X}})
of
x
for every
(x,\ell^{\prime})
with
x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}
and
\ell^{\prime}=1,\ldots,k
.

Assumption 16
.

There exists a
\widetilde{h}
such that for every compact set
K\in\mathcal{X}
, the density function
p((x,\ell),h,(y,\ell^{\prime}))
is bounded for
x\in K
,
y\in\mathcal{X}
,
\ell,\ell^{\prime}=1,\ldots,k
.

Lemma B.10
.

Assume that Assumption
13
–
16
hold. Then, all compact sets are petite.

Proof of Lemma
B.10
.

Define the joint stochastic processes
W(\cdot)=(Z(\cdot),X(\cdot))
and the underlying
\widetilde{h}
-skeleton processes

W^{(\widetilde{h})}=(Z^{(\widetilde{h})},X^{(\widetilde{h})})
for some
\widetilde{h}>0
.
From Theorem 2.18 in
Yin and Zhu (2010)
, under Assumption
13
–
14
, it follows that
W(\cdot)
is weak-Feller. Hence, the
\widetilde{h}
-skeleton
W^{(\widetilde{h})}
is also weak-Feller.

Next, we want to verify that the
\varphi
-irreducibility of
W^{(\widetilde{h})}
for some finite measure
\varphi
whose support has non-empty interior. To check this property, under Assumption
15
, we can apply Lemma
B.5
, which leads to verifying that
W^{(\widetilde{h})}(\cdot)
is a
T
-chain.

We want to verify that
W^{(\widetilde{h})}(\cdot)
is a
T
-chain with
T(\cdot,(A,\ell))=p(\cdot,\widetilde{h},(A,\ell))
,where
A\in\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{X})
and
\ell=1,\ldots,k
. To this end, it suffices to check the two conditions in Lemma
B.4
. Note that, by the weak-Feller condition,
p(\cdot,\widetilde{h},(O,\ell))
is lower semicontinuous (see
Meyn and Tweedie (2012, Chapter 6)
) for every open set
O\in\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{X})
. Hence the second condition of Lemma
B.4
is verified.

Given a compact set
K_{1}\in\mathcal{X}
, let
C
be a constant depending on
K_{1}
. Let
\mu
be a measure on
\mathcal{X}
. To verify the first condition, under Assumption
16
, we have

\sup_{x\in\mathcal{K}_{1},x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X},\ell,\ell^{\prime}=1,\ldots,k}p((x,\ell),\widetilde{h},(x^{\prime},\ell^{\prime}))\leq C.

Let
\delta=\varepsilon/C
. For any compact set
K_{2}
such that
A\subset K_{2}
and
\mu(A)<\delta
, then

\sup_{x\in\mathcal{K}_{1},\ell,\ell^{\prime}=1,\ldots,k}p((x,\ell),\widetilde{h},(A,\ell^{\prime}))=\sup_{x\in\mathcal{K}_{1},\ell,\ell^{\prime}=1,\ldots,k}\int_{A}p((x,\ell),\widetilde{h},(\mathrm{d}y,\ell^{\prime}))\leq C\mu(A)<\varepsilon.

Then, we have verified the first condition of Lemma
B.4
.

To summarize, under Assumption
15
–
16
,

W^{(\widetilde{h})}
is a T-chain, defined in Definition
14
, corresponding to a continuous component
p(\cdot,\widetilde{h},\cdot)
. Then, applying Lemma
B.5
, we can conclude that
W^{(\widetilde{h})}
is
p((x,\ell)),\widetilde{h},\cdot)
-irreducible for every
(x,\ell)\in\mathcal{X}\times[k]
. Finally, applying Lemma
B.3
, all compact sets are petite. ∎

B.5
Proof of Proposition
2.1

Let
p(\cdot,s,\cdot)
be the transition function, and
\eta
be the initial distribution of
(X(t),Z(t))
.

Assumption 17
.

There exist positive constants
\upsilon_{\ell}
for
\ell=1,\ldots,k
.
such that

\sup_{s\in\mathbb{R}_{+}}\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}\int(\upsilon_{\ell}\|x\|_{2}^{2}+1)\eta p(\cdot,s,(\mathrm{d}x,\ell))<\infty,

Proof of Proposition
2.1
.

The analysis is similar to the proof of Proposition
2.2
. In the first step, under Assumption
13
–
14
,
15
–
16
, we can apply Lemma
B.10
. The second step is to apply Lemma
B.2
using the result from Lemma
B.9
and Assumption
17
. The third step is the same as
Step 3
in the proof of Proposition
2.2
. Then, we complete the proof.

∎

B.6
Proof of Proposition
2.2

Recall that
p(\cdot,s,\cdot)
is the transition function, and
\eta
is the initial distribution of
(X(t),Z(t))
.

Assumption 18
.

There exists a constant
\gamma\in(0,1)
such that
1-\gamma c_{\ell}>0
for
\ell=1,\ldots,k
.
There exists a positive definite matrix
G
such that

\sup_{s\in\mathbb{R}_{+}}\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}\int\left\{(1-\gamma c_{\ell})(x^{\top}G^{2}x)^{\gamma/2}+1\right\}\eta p(\cdot,s,(\mathrm{d}x,\ell))<\infty.

Assumption
18
is the technical assumption required to verify the Condition
2
in Lemma
B.1
.

Proof of Proposition
2.2
.

Our proof consists of three steps. The first two steps are to show the requirements for Lemma
B.2
are fulfilled under the conditions stated in Proposition
2.2
.

Step 1
.
Since the diffusion process is linear,
\sum_{i}\theta_{i}^{\ell\star}g(X_{i}(t))=A_{\ell}
, Assumption
13
–
14
are satisfied.
Together with Assumption
15
–
16
, we can apply Lemma
B.10
. This completes showing the first requirement of Lemma
B.2
: all compact sets are petite.

Step 2
.
Using the results from Lemma
B.8
and
Step 1
, it follows from Lemma
B.2
that

\|p((x,\ell),t,\cdot)-\pi\|_{TV}\leq\|p((x,\ell),t,\cdot)-\pi\|_{V+1}\leq C(V(x,\ell)+1)\exp(-ct),\quad t\geq 0.

(B.7)

for
C>0
and
c>0
. Combining (
B.7
) with Assumption
18
, we can apply Lemma
B.1
. Hence, we see that

(Z(t),X(t))
is exponentially
\beta
-mixing.

Step 3
. Since
(Z(t),X(t))
is exponentially
\beta
-mixing, it follows that the discrete sampled process is
\beta
-mixing as well. Then, we can apply Lemma
B.7
, and show that the joint process
(Z(t_{n}),X(t_{n}),Y_{n})
is
\beta
-mixing and hence we complete the proof.
∎

Appendix C
Proof of Proposition
4.2

By the mean value theorem, we have

\displaystyle\left|M_{\sigma^{2}}(\Theta)-\sigma^{\star 2}\right|

\displaystyle=\left|M_{\sigma^{2}}(\Theta)-M_{\sigma^{2}}(\Theta^{\star})\right|\leq\kappa\left|\sigma^{2}-\sigma^{2\star}\right|;

\displaystyle\left|M_{q_{\ell\ell^{\prime}}}(\Theta)-q_{\ell\ell^{\prime}}^{\star}\right|

\displaystyle=\left|M_{q_{\ell\ell^{\prime}}}(\Theta)-M_{q_{\ell\ell^{\prime}}}(\Theta^{\star})\right|\leq\kappa\left|q_{\ell\ell^{\prime}}-q_{\ell\ell^{\prime}}^{\star}\right|\quad\ell\neq\ell^{\prime}=1,\ldots,k;

\displaystyle\left\|M_{\theta_{i\cdot}^{\ell}}(\Theta)-\theta_{i\cdot}^{\ell\star}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle=\left\|M_{\theta_{i\cdot}^{\ell}}(\Theta)-M_{\theta_{i\cdot}^{\ell}}(\Theta^{\star})\right\|_{2}\leq\kappa\|\theta_{i\cdot}^{\ell}-\theta_{i\cdot}^{\ell\star}\|_{2}\quad i=1,\ldots,p,\;\ell=1,\ldots,k.

Therefore, we have

\dist(M(\Theta),\Theta^{\star})\leq\kappa\dist(\Theta,\Theta^{\star}).

Appendix D
Truncated Continuous-time Markov Chain

Z(t_{1})
Z(t_{2})
Z(t_{3})
Z(t_{4})
Y_{1}
Y_{2}
Y_{3}
Y_{4}

(a)

Hidden Markov Model discussed in
(
Yang et al., 2017
)

Z(t_{1})
Z(t_{2})
Z(t_{3})
Z(t_{4})
Y_{1}
Y_{2}
Y_{3}
Y_{4}

(b)

Proposed Model

Figure 6
:
Two Hidden Markov Models

In this section, we will show that under the mixing condition, the filtered/smoothing probabilities are close to the truncated filtered/smoothing probabilities in total variation distance. Then, in the later section, we will establish the statistical guarantees on the truncated filtered/smoothing probabilities. We can view the sequences of filtered/smoothing probabilities as discrete-time stochastic processes taking values in
[0,1]
. The reason of performing an additional truncation step is because the long-range dependence of such processes poses challenge in establishing statistical properties like restricted eigenvalue condition and the deviation bound. In contrast, by construction, the truncated filtered/smoothing processes are mixing, whose concentration bounds for sample mean is known
(
Yu, 1994
;
Merlevède et al., 2011
)
.

Our proof techniques are built upon
van Handel (2008)
and
Yang et al. (2017)
where we extend and prior results to joint conditional processes
P(Z(t_{n}),Z(t_{n+1})\mid Y_{0},\ldots,Y_{N})
for
n=1,\ldots,N
. Since the structures of the Hidden Markov model discussed in
Yang et al. (2017)
is different than ours, as shown in Figure
6
, it requires new analysis to show the mixing property. In Appendix
D.1
–
D.2
, we respectively define the forward operator and the backward operator. Then using these operators, we can analyze the properties of truncated smoothing probabilities in Appendix
D.3
.

D.1
Truncated Forward Probability

For shorthand of notation we define
Z_{n}=Z(t_{n})
for
n=1,\ldots,N
and
Y_{n_{1}}^{n_{2}}=\{Y_{n_{1}},\ldots,Y_{n_{2}}\}
for
n_{1}<n_{2}
. Recall that the transition matrix is
P=e^{Qh}
with
i,j
-th entry being
P_{i,j}
for
i,j=1,\ldots,k
. Following similarly to the technique developed in Chapter 5 of
van Handel (2008)
, we define
F_{n}
as the operator of the
n
-th iteration for
n\geq 1
:

(F_{n}\nu)(j)=\frac{\sum_{i}P(Y_{n}\mid Z_{n}=j,Y_{n-1})P_{ij}\nu(i)}{\sum_{i,j}P(Y_{n}\mid Z_{n}=j,Y_{n-1})P_{ij}\nu(i)}

Then, we can express the filtered probability as

\displaystyle P(Z_{n}=j\mid Y_{0}^{n})

\displaystyle=\frac{\sum_{i}P(Y_{n}\mid Z_{n}=j,Y_{n-1})P_{ij}P(Z_{n-1}=i\mid Y_{0}^{n-1})}{\sum_{i,j}P(Y_{n}\mid Z_{n}=j,Y_{n-1})P_{ij}P(Z_{n-1}=i\mid Y_{0}^{n-1})}

\displaystyle=F_{n}P(Z_{n-1}\mid Y_{0}^{n-1})(j).

Iterate for
n-1
times, we obtain
P(Z_{n}=j\mid Y_{0}^{n})=F_{n}\cdots F_{2}P(Z_{1}\mid Y_{0}^{1})
.

Additionally, for
n>\ell\geq 1
, we define the transition kernel operator as

\displaystyle K_{\ell\mid n}(i,j)

\displaystyle=P(Z_{\ell}=j\mid Z_{\ell-1}=i,Y_{\ell-1}^{n}).

Then, for
n^{\prime}<\ell\leq n
we have

P(Z_{\ell}=j\mid Y_{n^{\prime}}^{n})=\sum_{i}K_{\ell\mid n}(i,j)P(Z_{\ell-1}=i\mid Y_{n^{\prime}}^{n}).

For
\ell^{\prime}\leq n
, we define

\nu_{\ell^{\prime}\mid n}=\frac{P(Y_{\ell^{\prime}+1}^{n}\mid Z_{\ell^{\prime}}=\cdot,Y_{\ell^{\prime}})\nu(\cdot)}{\sum_{j}P(Y_{\ell^{\prime}+1}^{n}\mid Z_{\ell^{\prime}}=j,Y_{\ell^{\prime}})\nu(j)}.

Define

ν
_ℓ’∣n^⊤K_ℓ’+1∣n=∑_i
ν
_ℓ’∣n(i)K_ℓ’+1∣n(i,⋅).

With simple computation, it leads to

F_{n}\cdots F_{\ell^{\prime}+1}\nu=\nu_{\ell^{\prime}\mid n}^{\top}K_{\ell^{\prime}+1\mid n}\cdots K_{n\mid n}.

The following lemma shows that the dependence of the filtered probability on the initial distribution decays geometrically.

Lemma D.1

(Adapted from Lemma 13 in
Yang et al. (2017)
)
.

If Assumption
9
is satisfied, then for probability distribution
\nu,\nu^{\prime}
and
n\geq\ell
, we have

\|F_{n}\cdots F_{\ell+1}(\nu-\nu^{\prime})\|_{\infty}\leq\zeta^{-2}(1-\zeta\pi_{\min})^{n-\ell}\|\nu-\nu^{\prime}\|_{1}.

Additionally, we have

\max_{i=1,\ldots,k}\left|p(Z_{n}=i\mid Y_{1}^{n})-p(Z_{n}=i\mid Y_{n-\ell}^{n})\right|\leq 2\zeta^{-2}(1-\zeta\pi_{\min})^{\ell}.

Proof of Lemma
D.1
.

The proof is similar to the proof of Lemma 13 in
Yang et al. (2017)
, except that the transition kernel operator and the forward operator defined in this manuscript are different from the ones introduced in
Yang et al. (2017)
. We study the case that the observation distribution
P(Y_{n}\mid Z_{n},Y_{n-1})
depends on the previous observation
Y_{n-1}
, whereas the observation distribution defined in
Yang et al. (2017)
is independent to the previous observation
Y_{n-1}
given
Z_{n}
.
Despite the differences, under Assumption
9
,
Yang et al. (2017)
showed that
K_{\ell\mid n}\geq(\zeta\pi_{\min})P_{\ell\mid n}
with

P_{\ell\mid n}=\frac{\pi(j)P(Y_{n+1}^{n^{\prime}}\mid Y_{n},Z_{n}=j)P(Y_{n}\mid Z_{n}=j,Y_{n-1})}{\sum_{j}\pi(j)P(Y_{n+1}^{n^{\prime}}\mid Y_{n},Z_{n}=j)P(Y_{n}\mid Z_{n}=j,Y_{n-1})}.

Hence, following the Doeblin minorization condition, we can decompose the transition kernel operator as

K_{\ell\mid n}=\zeta\pi_{\min}P_{\ell\mid n}+(1-\zeta\pi_{\min})Q_{\ell\mid n},

where
Q_{\ell\mid n}
is some transition kernel operator.
Then, the rest follows similarly to the proof of Lemma 13 in
Yang et al. (2017)
.
∎

The mixing property, Assumption
9
also guarantees that the conditional probability
P(Y_{\ell+1}^{n}\mid Z_{\ell}=\cdot,Y_{\ell})
is well-behaved.

Lemma D.2
.

Under Assumption
9
and
n\geq\ell
, the following inequality holds:

\frac{\max_{i}P(Y_{\ell+1}^{n}\mid Z_{\ell}=i,Y_{\ell})}{\min_{i}P(Y_{\ell+1}^{n}\mid Z_{\ell}=i,Y_{\ell})}\leq\zeta^{-2}.

Proof of Lemma
D.2
.

Write

\displaystyle P(Y_{\ell+1}^{n}\mid Z_{\ell}=i,Y_{\ell})

\displaystyle=\sum_{z_{n},\ldots,z_{\ell+1}}P(Y_{n}\mid z_{n},Y_{n-1})P(z_{n}\mid z_{n-1})\cdots P(Y_{\ell+1}\mid z_{\ell+1},Y_{\ell})P(z_{\ell+1}\mid Z_{\ell}=i)

\displaystyle\leq\zeta^{-1}\sum_{z_{n},\ldots,z_{\ell+1}}P(Y_{n}\mid z_{n},Y_{n-1})P(z_{n}\mid z_{n-1})\cdots P(Y_{\ell+1}\mid z_{\ell+1},Y_{\ell})\pi(z_{\ell+1}),

(D.1)

where the last line follows from Assumption
9
. Similarly, we have

\displaystyle P(Y_{\ell+1}^{n}\mid Z_{\ell}=i,Y_{\ell})\geq\zeta\sum_{z_{n},\ldots,z_{\ell+1}}P(Y_{n}\mid z_{n},Y_{n-1})P(z_{n}\mid z_{n-1})\cdots P(Y_{\ell+1}\mid z_{\ell+1},Y_{\ell})\pi(z_{\ell+1})

(D.2)

Taking the maximum with respect to
i
on the left side of (
D.1
) and minimum with respect to
i
on the left side of (
D.2
), we can conclude that

\frac{\max_{i}P(Y_{\ell+1}^{n}\mid Z_{\ell}=i,Y_{\ell})}{\min_{i}P(Y_{\ell+1}^{n}\mid Z_{\ell}=i,Y_{\ell})}\leq\zeta^{-2}.

∎

D.2
Truncated Backward Probability

To define the backward recursion, we first look at

\displaystyle P(Z_{n}\mid Z_{n+1},Y_{n})

\displaystyle=\frac{P(Z_{n}\mid Y_{n})P(Z_{n+1}\mid Z_{n},Y_{n})}{P(Z_{n+1}\mid Y_{n})}=\frac{P(Z_{n}\mid Y_{n})P(Z_{n+1}\mid Z_{n})}{P(Z_{n+1}\mid Y_{n})},

where the equality follows by the fact that
Z_{n+1}\perp\!\!\!\perp Y_{n}\mid Z_{n}
.
With this equality, let
N\geq n^{\prime}>n
, we have

\displaystyle P(Y_{n+1}^{n^{\prime}},Y_{n},Z_{n+1},Z_{n})

\displaystyle=P(Z_{n}\mid Z_{n+1},Y_{n})P(Y_{n}\mid Y_{n+1},Z_{n+1})P(Y_{n+1}^{n^{\prime}},Z_{n+1})

\displaystyle=\frac{P(Z_{n}\mid Y_{n})P(Z_{n+1}\mid Z_{n})}{P(Z_{n+1}\mid Y_{n})}P(Y_{n}\mid Z_{n+1},Y_{n+1})P(Y_{n+1}^{n^{\prime}},Z_{n+1}).

Therefore, we can write the backward recursion formula as

\displaystyle P(Z_{n}=i\mid Y_{n}^{n^{\prime}})=\frac{\sum_{j}\frac{P(Z_{n}=i\mid Y_{n})}{P(Z_{n+1}=j\mid Y_{n})}P(Y_{n}\mid Z_{n+1}=j,Y_{n+1})P_{ij}P(Z_{n+1}=j\mid Y_{n+1}^{n^{\prime}})}{\sum_{i,j}\frac{P(Z_{n}=i\mid Y_{n})}{P(Z_{n+1}=j\mid Y_{n})}P(Y_{n}\mid Z_{n+1}=j,Y_{n+1})P_{ij}P(Z_{n+1}=j\mid Y_{n+1}^{n^{\prime}})}.

Hence, we define
\widetilde{F}_{n}
similarly as
F_{n}
:

(\widetilde{F}_{n}\widetilde{\nu})(i)=\frac{\sum_{j}\frac{P(Z_{n}=i\mid Y_{n})}{P(Z_{n+1}=j\mid Y_{n})}P_{ij}P(Y_{n}\mid Z_{n+1}=j,Y_{n+1})\widetilde{\nu}(j)}{\sum_{i,j}\frac{P(Z_{n}=i\mid Y_{n})}{P(Z_{n+1}=j\mid Y_{n})}P_{ij}P(Y_{n}\mid Z_{n+1}=j,Y_{n+1})\widetilde{\nu}(j)}.

We immediately get
P(Z_{n}\mid Y_{n}^{N})=\widetilde{F}_{n}\cdots\widetilde{F}_{N-1}P(Z_{N}\mid Y_{N})
.
Additionally, for
n\leq\ell\leq n^{\prime}
, we define the backward transition kernel operator as

\widetilde{K}_{n\mid\ell}(j,i)=\frac{P_{ij}P(Y_{\ell+1}\mid Z_{\ell+1}=j,Y_{\ell})P(Z_{\ell}=i,Y_{n}^{\ell})}{\sum_{i}P_{ij}P(Y_{\ell+1}\mid Z_{\ell+1}=j,Y_{\ell})P(Z_{\ell}=i,Y_{n}^{\ell})}=P(Z_{\ell}=i\mid Z_{\ell+1}=j,Y_{n}^{\ell+1}),

which leads to

P(Z_{\ell}\mid Y_{n}^{n^{\prime}})=\sum_{j}\widetilde{K}_{n\mid\ell}(j,\cdot)P(Z_{\ell+1}=j\mid Y_{n}^{n^{\prime}}).

More generally, define

\widetilde{\nu}_{n\mid\ell}=\frac{P(Y_{n}^{\ell-1}\mid Z_{\ell}=\cdot,Y_{\ell})\widetilde{\nu}(\cdot)}{\sum_{j}P(Y_{n}^{\ell-1}\mid Z_{\ell}=j,Y_{\ell})\widetilde{\nu}(j)}.

Then, we define

\widetilde{\nu}_{n\mid\ell}^{\top}\widetilde{K}_{n\mid\ell-1}=\sum_{j}\widetilde{\nu}_{n\mid\ell}(j)\widetilde{K}_{n\mid\ell-1}(j,\cdot).

Finally, we have

\widetilde{F}_{n}\cdots\widetilde{F}_{\ell-1}\widetilde{\nu}=\widetilde{\nu}_{n\mid\ell}^{\top}\widetilde{K}_{n\mid\ell-1}\cdots\widetilde{K}_{n\mid n}.

(D.3)

Lemma D.3
.

Under Assumption
8
,
9
, and for any probability distributions
\widetilde{\nu},\widetilde{\mu}
and
n\leq\ell
, we have

\|\widetilde{F}_{n}\cdots\widetilde{F}_{\ell-1}(\widetilde{\nu}-\widetilde{\mu})\|_{\infty}\leq 2(\pi_{\min}\zeta)^{-2}\left\{1-(\pi_{\min}\zeta)^{2}\right\}^{\ell-n}\|\widetilde{\nu}-\widetilde{\mu}\|_{1}.

Furthermore,

\max_{i=1,\ldots,k}\left|p(Z_{n}=i\mid Y_{n}^{N})-p(Z_{n}=i\mid Y_{n}^{\ell})\right|\leq 4(\pi_{\min}\zeta)^{-2}\left\{1-(\pi_{\min}\zeta)^{2}\right\}^{\ell-n}.

Proof of Lemma
D.3
.

The proof follows similarly to the proof of Lemma
D.1
. To show the first statement, it suffices to show the contraction of the transition kernel operator
\widetilde{K}_{n\mid\ell}
following (
D.3
). Under Assumption
9
–
8
, we have

\zeta\pi_{\min}\pi(i)\leq P_{ij}\leq(\zeta\pi_{\min})^{-1}\pi(i).

(D.4)

With this fact,
\widetilde{K}_{n\mid\ell}
is lower bounded by

\displaystyle\widetilde{K}_{n\mid\ell}(j,i)

\displaystyle=\frac{P_{ij}P(Y_{\ell+1}\mid Z_{\ell}=i,Y_{\ell})P(Z_{\ell}=i,Y_{n}^{\ell})}{\sum_{i}P_{ij}P(Y_{\ell+1}\mid Z_{\ell}=i,Y_{\ell})P(Z_{\ell}=i,Y_{n}^{\ell})}

\displaystyle\geq(\zeta\pi_{\min})^{2}\frac{\pi(i)P(Y_{\ell+1}\mid Z_{\ell}=i,Y_{\ell})P(Z_{\ell}=i,Y_{n}^{\ell})}{\sum_{i}\pi(i)P(Y_{\ell+1}\mid Z_{\ell}=i,Y_{\ell})P(Z_{\ell}=i,Y_{n}^{\ell})}.

Define

~

P

_n∣ℓ(j, i)=

π
(i)P(Y

ℓ+1

∣Z

ℓ

=i, Y

ℓ

)P(Z

ℓ

=i, Y

n

ℓ

)

∑

i

π
(i)P(Y

ℓ+1

∣Z

ℓ

=i, Y

ℓ

)P(Z

ℓ

=i, Y

n

ℓ

)

,

for all
i,j=1,\ldots,k
. Then, we can write

\widetilde{K}_{n\mid\ell}=(\zeta\pi_{\min})^{2}\widetilde{P}_{n\mid\ell}+\left\{1-(\zeta\pi_{\min})^{2}\right\}\widetilde{Q}_{n\mid\ell},

where
\widetilde{Q}_{n\mid\ell}
is a transition kernel operator.

Note that for any
\widetilde{\nu},\widetilde{\mu}
, we have
\widetilde{\nu}_{n\mid\ell+1}^{\top}\widetilde{P}_{n\mid\ell}=\widetilde{\mu}_{n\mid\ell+1}^{\top}\widetilde{P}_{n\mid\ell}
.
Iterate over
n-\ell
iterations, we have

\widetilde{F}_{n}\cdots\widetilde{F}_{\ell-1}(\widetilde{\nu}-\widetilde{\mu})=\left\{1-(\zeta\pi_{\min})^{2}\right\}^{\ell-n}(\widetilde{\nu}_{n\mid\ell}-\widetilde{\mu}_{n\mid\ell})^{\top}\widetilde{Q}_{n\mid\ell-1}\cdots\widetilde{Q}_{n\mid n}.

It follows that

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{F}_{n}\cdots\widetilde{F}_{\ell-1}(\widetilde{\nu}-\widetilde{\mu})\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle=\left\{1-(\zeta\pi_{\min})^{2}\right\}^{\ell-n}\|(\widetilde{\nu}_{n\mid\ell}-\widetilde{\mu}_{n\mid\ell})^{\top}\widetilde{Q}_{n\mid\ell-1}\cdots\widetilde{Q}_{n\mid n}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\left\{1-(\zeta\pi_{\min})^{2}\right\}^{\ell-n}\|\widetilde{\nu}_{n\mid\ell}-\widetilde{\mu}_{n\mid\ell}\|_{2}\prod_{i=n}^{\ell}\|\widetilde{Q}_{n\mid i}\|_{2}.

Since
\widetilde{Q}_{n\mid\ell}
is a transition kernel operator, it follows that
\|\widetilde{Q}_{n\mid\ell}\|_{2}\leq 1
for all
i=n,\ldots,\ell
. Then, we can further bound the above display as

\displaystyle\leq\left\{1-(\zeta\pi_{\min})^{2}\right\}^{\ell-n}\|\widetilde{\nu}_{n\mid\ell}-\widetilde{\mu}_{n\mid\ell}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left\{1-(\zeta\pi_{\min})^{2}\right\}^{\ell-n}\bigg\{\bigg\|\frac{P(Y_{n}^{\ell-1}\mid Z_{\ell}=\cdot,Y_{\ell})}{\sum_{j}P(Y_{n}^{\ell-1}\mid Z_{\ell}=j,Y_{\ell})\widetilde{\nu}(j)}(\widetilde{\nu}(\cdot)-\widetilde{\mu}(\cdot))\bigg\|_{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\left(\frac{1}{\sum_{j}P(Y_{n}^{\ell-1}\mid Z_{\ell}=j,Y_{\ell})\widetilde{\nu}(j)}-\frac{1}{\sum_{j}P(Y_{n}^{\ell-1}\mid Z_{\ell}=j,Y_{\ell})\widetilde{\mu}(j)}\right)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\times\|{P(Y_{n}^{\ell-1}\mid Z_{\ell}=\cdot,Y_{\ell})\widetilde{\mu}(\cdot)}\|_{2}\bigg\}.

(D.5)

Note that

\displaystyle\bigg\|\frac{P(Y_{n}^{\ell-1}\mid Z_{\ell}=\cdot,Y_{\ell})}{\sum_{j}P(Y_{n}^{\ell-1}\mid Z_{\ell}=j,Y_{\ell})\widetilde{\nu}(j)}(\widetilde{\nu}(\cdot)-\widetilde{\mu}(\cdot))\bigg\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\bigg\|\frac{P(Y_{n}^{\ell-1}\mid Z_{\ell}=\cdot,Y_{\ell})}{\sum_{j}P(Y_{n}^{\ell-1}\mid Z_{\ell}=j,Y_{\ell})\widetilde{\nu}(j)}(\widetilde{\nu}(\cdot)-\widetilde{\mu}(\cdot))\bigg\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\max_{i}P(Y_{n}^{\ell-1}\mid Z_{\ell}=i,Y_{\ell})}{\min_{i}P(Y_{n}^{\ell-1}\mid Z_{\ell}=i,Y_{\ell})}\|\widetilde{\nu}-\widetilde{\mu}\|_{1}.

(D.6)

Furthermore,

\displaystyle\left(\frac{1}{\sum_{j}P(Y_{n}^{\ell-1}\mid Z_{\ell}=j,Y_{\ell})\widetilde{\nu}(j)}-\frac{1}{\sum_{j}P(Y_{n}^{\ell-1}\mid Z_{\ell}=j,Y_{\ell})\widetilde{\mu}(j)}\right)\|P(Y_{n}^{\ell-1}\mid Z_{\ell}=\cdot,Y_{\ell})\widetilde{\mu}(\cdot)\|_{2}.

Applying the fact that
{\|P(Y_{n}^{\ell-1}\mid Z_{\ell}=\cdot,Y_{\ell})\widetilde{\mu}(\cdot)\|_{2}}\leq{\|P(Y_{n}^{\ell-1}\mid Z_{\ell}=\cdot,Y_{\ell})\widetilde{\mu}(\cdot)\|_{1}}
, we can further bound the above display as

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\leq\frac{1}{\sum_{j}P(Y_{n}^{\ell-1}\mid Z_{\ell}=j,Y_{\ell})\widetilde{\nu}(j)}\left(\sum_{j}P(Y_{n}^{\ell-1}\mid Z_{\ell}=j,Y_{\ell})\widetilde{\mu}(j)-\sum_{j}P(Y_{n}^{\ell-1}\mid Z_{\ell}=j,Y_{\ell})\widetilde{\nu}(j)\right)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\leq\frac{\max_{i}P(Y_{n}^{\ell-1}\mid Z_{\ell}=i,Y_{\ell})}{\min_{i}P(Y_{n}^{\ell-1}\mid Z_{\ell}=i,Y_{\ell})}\|\widetilde{\mu}-\widetilde{\nu}\|_{1}.

(D.7)

Plugging (
D.6
)– (
D.7
) into (
D.5
), we arrive at

\|\widetilde{F}_{n}\cdots\widetilde{F}_{\ell-1}(\widetilde{\nu}-\widetilde{\mu})\|\leq 2\left\{1-(\zeta\pi_{\min})^{2}\right\}^{\ell-n}\bigg(\frac{\max_{i}P(Y_{n}^{\ell-1}\mid Z_{\ell}=i,Y_{\ell})}{\min_{i}P(Y_{n}^{\ell-1}\mid Z_{\ell}=i,Y_{\ell})}\|\widetilde{\nu}-\widetilde{\mu}\|_{1}\bigg).

(D.8)

Note that we have
(\zeta\pi_{\min})C_{0}\leq P(Y_{n}^{\ell-1}\mid Z_{\ell}=i,Y_{\ell})\leq(\zeta\pi_{\min})^{-1}C_{0}
,
where

C_{0}=\sum_{z_{n},\ldots,z_{\ell-1}}P(z_{n}\mid z_{n+1},Y_{n})P(Y_{n}\mid Y_{n+1},z_{n+1})\cdots P(z_{\ell-2}\mid z_{\ell-1},Y_{\ell-2})P(Y_{\ell-2}\mid z_{\ell-1},Y_{\ell-1}\\
\times\frac{P(z_{\ell-1}\mid Y_{\ell-1})}{P(z_{\ell}\mid Y_{\ell-1})}P(Y_{\ell-1}\mid Z_{\ell}=i,Y_{\ell})\pi(i).

Hence,

\frac{\max_{i}P(Y_{n}^{\ell-1}\mid Z_{\ell}=i,Y_{\ell})}{\min_{i}P(Y_{n}^{\ell-1}\mid Z_{\ell}=i,Y_{\ell})}\leq(\zeta\pi_{\min})^{-2}.

Applying the above inequality to (
D.8
), we conclude the first statement. The second statement is shown by choosing
\widetilde{\nu}=P(Z_{\ell}\mid Y_{\ell})
,
\widetilde{\mu}=P(Z_{\ell}\mid Y_{\ell}^{N})
and using the fact that
\|\widetilde{\nu}-\widetilde{\mu}\|_{1}\leq 2
.
∎

D.3
Truncated Smoothing Probability

Proof of Lemma
4.4
.

First, we can express

\displaystyle P\bigg(Z_{n},Z_{n+1}

\displaystyle,Y_{(n-r)\vee 0}^{(n+r)\wedge N}\bigg)

\displaystyle=P\left(Z_{n},Y_{(n-r)\vee 0}^{n}\right)P(Z_{n+1}\mid Z_{n})P(Y_{n+1}\mid Z_{n+1},Y_{n})P\left(Y_{n+2}^{(n+r)\wedge N}\mid Z_{n+1},Y_{n+1}\right)

\displaystyle=P\left(Z_{n}\mid Y_{(n-r)\vee 0}^{n}\right)P\left(Z_{n+1}\mid Y_{n+1}^{(n+r)\wedge N}\right)P(Z_{n+1}\mid Z_{n})P(Y_{n+1}\mid Z_{n+1},Y_{n})

\displaystyle\quad\times\frac{P\left(Y_{n+1}^{(n+r)\wedge N}\right)P\left(Y_{(n-r)\vee 0}^{n}\right)}{P(Z_{n+1},Y_{n+1})}.

This implies that

\displaystyle P\bigg(

\displaystyle Z_{n}=i,Z_{n+1}=j\mid Y_{(n-r)\vee 0}^{(n+r)\wedge N}\bigg)

\displaystyle=P\left(Z_{n}\mid Y_{(n-r)\vee 0}^{n}\right)P\left(Z_{n+1}\mid Y_{n+1}^{(n+r)\wedge N}\right)\frac{P\left(Y_{n+1}^{(n+r)\wedge N}\mid Y_{n}\right)}{P\left(Y_{n+1}^{(n+r)\wedge N}\mid Y_{(n-r)\vee 0}^{n}\right)}\frac{P_{ij}P(Y_{n+1}\mid Z_{n+1},Y_{n})P(Y_{n})}{P(Z_{n+1},Y_{n+1})}.

Similarly, we can write

\displaystyle P\bigg(

\displaystyle Z_{n}=i,Z_{n+1}=j\mid Y_{1}^{N}\bigg)=P\left(Z_{n}\mid Y_{1}^{n}\right)P\left(Z_{n+1}\mid Y_{n+1}^{N}\right)\frac{P\left(Y_{n+1}^{N}\mid Y_{n}\right)}{P\left(Y_{n+1}^{N}\mid Y_{0}^{n}\right)}\frac{P_{ij}P(Y_{n+1}\mid Z_{n+1},Y_{n})P(Y_{n})}{P(Z_{n+1},Y_{n+1})}.

Define
a_{ij}={P_{ij}P(Y_{n+1}\mid Z_{n+1},Y_{n})}P(Y_{n})/{P(Z_{n+1},Y_{n+1})}
for
i,j=1,\ldots,k
and

B=\left|\frac{P(Y_{n+1}^{N})P(Y_{0}^{n})}{P(Y_{0}^{N})}\frac{P\left(Y_{(n-r)\vee 0}^{(n+r)\wedge N}\right)}{P\left(Y_{n+1}^{(n+r)\wedge N}\right)P\left(Y_{(n-r)\vee 0}^{n}\right)}-1\right|

(D.9)

Hence, we have

\displaystyle\left|P\bigg(Z_{n}=i,Z_{n+1}=j\mid Y_{(n-r)\vee 0}^{(n+r)\wedge N}\bigg)-P\bigg(Z_{n}=i,Z_{n+1}=j\mid Y_{1}^{N}\bigg)\right|

\displaystyle\quad\leq a_{ij}\Bigg\{\frac{P(Y_{n+1}^{N}\mid Y_{n})}{P(Y_{n+1}^{N}\mid Y_{0}^{n})}\left|P(Z_{n}\mid Y_{0}^{n})P(Z_{n+1}\mid Y_{n+1}^{N})-P\left(Z_{n}\mid Y_{(n-r)\vee 0}^{n}\right)P\left(Z_{n+1}\mid Y_{n+1}^{(n+r)\wedge N}\right)\right|

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad+B\frac{P\left(Y_{n+1}^{(n+r)\wedge N}\mid Y_{n}\right)}{P\left(Y_{n+1}^{(n+r)\wedge N}\mid Y_{(n-r)\vee 0}^{n}\right)}P\left(Z_{n}\mid Y_{(n-r)\vee 0}^{n}\right)P\left(Z_{n+1}\mid Y_{n+1}^{(n+r)\wedge N}\right)\Bigg\}

\displaystyle\quad\leq\delta_{\min}^{-1}\Bigg\{\frac{P(Y_{n+1}^{N}\mid Y_{n})}{P(Y_{n+1}^{N}\mid Y_{0}^{n})}\left|P(Z_{n}\mid Y_{0}^{n})P(Z_{n+1}\mid Y_{n+1}^{N})-P\left(Z_{n}\mid Y_{(n-r)\vee 0}^{n}\right)P\left(Z_{n+1}\mid Y_{n+1}^{(n+r)\wedge N}\right)\right|

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad+B\frac{P\left(Y_{n+1}^{(n+r)\wedge N}\mid Y_{n}\right)}{P\left(Y_{n+1}^{(n+r)\wedge N}\mid Y_{(n-r)\vee 0}^{n}\right)}P\left(Z_{n}\mid Y_{(n-r)\vee 0}^{n}\right)P\left(Z_{n+1}\mid Y_{n+1}^{(n+r)\wedge N}\right)\Bigg\}.

(D.10)

First we note that

\displaystyle\frac{P(Y_{n+1}^{N})}{P(Y_{n+1}^{N}\mid Y_{0}^{n})}=\frac{\sum_{i}P(Y_{n+1}^{N}\mid Z_{n}=i,Y_{n})P(Z_{n}=i\mid Y_{n})}{\sum_{i}P(Y_{n+1}^{N}\mid Z_{n}=i,Y_{n})P(Z_{n}=i\mid Y_{0}^{n})}\leq\frac{\max_{i}P(Y_{n+1}^{N}\mid Z_{n}=i,Y_{n})}{\min_{i}P(Y_{n+1}^{N}\mid Z_{n}=i,Y_{n})}\leq\zeta^{-2},

(D.11)

where the last inequality follows Lemma
D.2
. Similarly, it can be shown that

\frac{P\left(Y_{n+1}^{(n+r)\wedge N}\mid Y_{n}\right)}{P\left(Y_{n+1}^{(n+r)\wedge N}\mid Y_{(n-r)\vee 0}^{n}\right)}\leq\zeta^{-2}.

(D.12)

Secondly, by Lemma
D.1
and Lemma
D.3
, we have

\displaystyle|P(Z_{n}\mid

\displaystyle Y_{0}^{n})P(Z_{n+1}\mid Y_{n+1}^{N})-P\left(Z_{n}\mid Y_{(n-r)\vee 0}^{n}\right)P\left(Z_{n+1}\mid Y_{n+1}^{(n+r)\wedge N}\right)|

\displaystyle\leq\left|P(Z_{n}\mid Y_{0}^{n})-P\left(Z_{n}\mid Y_{(n-r)\vee 0}^{n}\right)\right|+\left|P(Z_{n+1}\mid Y_{n+1}^{N})-P\left(Z_{n+1}\mid Y_{n+1}^{(n+r)\wedge N}\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leq 2\zeta^{-2}(1-\zeta\pi_{\min})^{r}+4(\zeta\pi_{\min})^{-2}\left\{1-(\zeta\pi_{\min})^{2}\right\}^{r-1}\leq 6(\zeta\pi_{\min})^{-2}\left\{1-(\zeta\pi_{\min})^{2}\right\}^{r-1}.

(D.13)

Collecting the results from (
D.11
)– (
D.13
) and Lemma
D.4
, we can bound (
D.10
) as

\left|P\bigg(Z_{n}=i,Z_{n+1}=j\mid Y_{(n-r)\vee 0}^{(n+r)\wedge N}\bigg)-P\bigg(Z_{n}=i,Z_{n+1}=j\mid Y_{1}^{N}\bigg)\right|\leq C\delta_{\min}^{-1}\zeta^{-8}\pi_{\min}^{-2}(1-(\zeta\pi_{\min})^{2})^{r-1},

where
C
is some constant.

To show the second statement, we can write

\displaystyle P(Z_{n}\mid Y_{0}^{N})=P(Z_{n}\mid Y_{n}^{N})P(Z_{n}\mid Y_{0}^{n})\frac{P(Y_{n+1}^{N}\mid Y_{n})}{P(Y_{n+1}^{N}\mid Y_{0}^{n})}\frac{1}{P(Z_{n}\mid Y_{n})}

Similarly, we have

\displaystyle P\left(Z_{n}\mid Y_{(n-r)\vee 0}^{(n+r)\wedge N}\right)=P(Z_{n}\mid Y_{n}^{(n+r)\wedge N})P(Z_{n}\mid Y_{(n-r)\vee 0}^{n})\frac{P(Y_{n+1}^{(n+r)\wedge N}\mid Y_{n})}{P(Y_{n+1}^{N}\mid Y_{(n-r)\vee 0}^{n})}\frac{1}{P(Z_{n}\mid Y_{n})}.

Hence, we can obtain the following upper bound

\displaystyle\bigg|P(Z_{n}\mid

\displaystyle Y_{0}^{N})-P\left(Z_{n}\mid Y_{(n-r)\vee 0}^{(n+r)\wedge N}\right)\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{P(Z_{n}\mid Y_{n})}\frac{P(Y_{n+1}^{N}\mid Y_{n})}{P(Y_{n+1}^{N}\mid Y_{0}^{n})}\left|P(Z_{n}\mid Y_{n}^{N})P(Z_{n}\mid Y_{0}^{n})-P(Z_{n}\mid Y_{n}^{(n+r)\wedge N})P(Z_{n}\mid Y_{(n-r)\vee 0}^{n})\right|

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{P(Z_{n}\mid Y_{n})}\frac{P(Y_{n+1}^{(n+r)\wedge N}\mid Y_{n})}{P(Y_{n+1}^{N}\mid Y_{(n-r)\vee 0}^{n})}BP(Z_{n}\mid Y_{n}^{(n+r)\wedge N})P(Z_{n}\mid Y_{(n-r)\vee 0}^{n}),

where
B
is defined in (
D.13
).
Note that we can write

\displaystyle\big|P(Z_{n}\mid Y_{n}^{N})P(Z_{n}\mid Y_{0}^{n})

\displaystyle-P(Z_{n}\mid Y_{n}^{(n+r)\wedge N})P(Z_{n}\mid Y_{(n-r)\vee 0}^{n})\big|

\displaystyle\leq\left|P(Z_{n}\mid Y_{n}^{N})-P(Z_{n}\mid Y_{n}^{(n+r)\wedge N})\right|+\left|P(Z_{n}\mid Y_{0}^{n})-P(Z_{n}\mid Y_{(n-r)\vee 0}^{n})\right|

\displaystyle\leq 4(\zeta\pi_{\min})^{-2}\left\{1-(\zeta\pi_{\min})^{2}\right\}^{r}+2\zeta^{-2}(1-\zeta\pi_{\min})^{r}

\displaystyle\leq 6(\zeta\pi_{\min})^{-2}\left\{1-(\zeta\pi_{\min})^{2}\right\}^{r}.

(D.14)

Collecting the results from (
D.11
)– (
D.12
), (
D.14
) and Lemma
D.4
, we can conclude that

\displaystyle\bigg|P(Z_{n}\mid Y_{0}^{N})-P\left(Z_{n}\mid Y_{(n-r)\vee 0}^{(n+r)\wedge N}\right)\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{10}{P(Z_{n}=i\mid Y_{n})}\zeta^{-8}\pi_{\min}^{-2}\left\{(1-(\zeta\pi_{\min})^{2})^{r}\vee(1-\zeta\pi_{\min})^{r-1}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{10}{\delta_{\min}}\zeta^{-8}\pi_{\min}^{-2}\left\{1-(\zeta\pi_{\min})^{2}\right\}^{r-1}

∎

Lemma D.4
.

Under Assumption
9
and given
r\in\mathbb{N}
, we have

\left|\frac{P\left(Y_{(n-r)\vee 0}^{(n+r)\wedge N}\right)}{P\left(Y_{n+1}^{(n+r)\wedge N}\right)P\left(Y_{(n-r)\vee 0}^{n}\right)}\frac{P(Y_{n+1}^{N})P(Y_{0}^{n})}{P(Y_{0}^{N})}-1\right|\leq 4\zeta^{-4}k(1-\zeta\pi_{\min})^{r-1}\left\{\zeta^{-2}(1-\zeta\pi_{\min})\vee 1\right\}.

(D.15)

Proof of Lemma
D.4
.

We consider four cases.

Case 1
:
n+r>N
,
n-r<0
. It follows that

\frac{P\left(Y_{(n-r)\vee 0}^{(n+r)\wedge N}\right)}{P\left(Y_{n+1}^{(n+r)\wedge N}\right)P\left(Y_{(n-r)\vee 0}^{n}\right)}\frac{P(Y_{n+1}^{N})P(Y_{0}^{n})}{P(Y_{0}^{N})}=1.

Case 2
:
n+r<N
,
n-r>0
. We can write the left hand side of (
D.15
) as

\displaystyle\left|\frac{P(Y_{n+1}^{n+r}\mid Y_{n-r}^{n})P(Y_{n+r+1}^{N}\mid Y_{n+1}^{n+r})}{P(Y_{n+1}^{N}\mid Y_{0}^{n})}-1\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\frac{P(Y_{n+1}^{n+r}\mid Y_{n-r}^{n})P(Y_{n+r+1}^{N}\mid Y_{n+1}^{n+r})}{P(Y_{n+r+1}^{N}\mid Y_{0}^{n+r})P(Y_{n+1}^{n+r}\mid Y_{0}^{n})}-1\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{P(Y_{n+r+1}^{N}\mid Y_{n+1}^{n+r})}{P(Y_{n+r+1}^{N}\mid Y_{0}^{n+r})}\left|\frac{P(Y_{n+1}^{n+r}\mid Y_{n-r}^{n})-P(Y_{n+1}^{n+r}\mid Y_{0}^{n})}{P(Y_{n+1}^{n+r}\mid Y_{0}^{n})}\right|

\displaystyle\quad+\left|\frac{P(Y_{n+r+1}^{N}\mid Y_{n+1}^{n+r})-P(Y_{n+r+1}^{N}\mid Y_{0}^{n+r})}{P(Y_{n+r+1}^{N}\mid Y_{0}^{n+r})}\right|.

(D.16)

We can write

\displaystyle\bigg|\frac{P(Y_{n+1}^{n+r}\mid Y_{n-r}^{n})-P(Y_{n+1}^{n+r}\mid Y_{0}^{n})}{P(Y_{n+1}^{n+r}\mid Y_{0}^{n})}\bigg|

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad=\frac{\left|\sum_{i}P(Y_{n+1}^{n+r}\mid Z_{n}=i,Y_{n})\left\{P(Z_{n}=i\mid Y_{n-r}^{n})-P(Z_{n}=i\mid Y_{1}^{n})\right\}\right|}{\sum_{i}P(Y_{n+1}^{n+r}\mid Z_{n}=i,Y_{n})P(Z_{n}=i\mid Y_{0}^{n})}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\leq\frac{\max_{i}P(Y_{n+1}^{n+r}\mid Z_{n}=i,Y_{n})}{\min_{i}P(Y_{n+1}^{n+r}\mid Z_{n}=i,Y_{n})}\left|\sum_{i}P(Z_{n}=i\mid Y_{n-r}^{n})-P(Z_{n}=i\mid Y_{1}^{n})\right|.

Apply Lemma
D.1
–
D.2
, we can upper bound the above display as

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\leq 2\zeta^{-4}k(1-\zeta\pi_{\min})^{r}

(D.17)

Similarly, we can use the same technique to show that

\displaystyle\left|\frac{P(Y_{n+r+1}^{N}\mid Y_{n+1}^{n+r})-P(Y_{n+r+1}^{N}\mid Y_{0}^{n+r})}{P(Y_{n+r+1}^{N}\mid Y_{0}^{n+r})}\right|\leq 2\zeta^{-4}k(1-\zeta\pi_{\min})^{r-1}.

(D.18)

Apply Lemma
D.2
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{P(Y_{n+r+1}^{N}\mid Y_{n+1}^{n+r})}{P(Y_{n+r+1}^{N}\mid Y_{0}^{n+r})}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sum_{i}P(Y_{n+r+1}^{N}\mid Z_{n}=i,Y_{n+r})P(Z_{n}=i\mid Y_{n+1}^{n+r})}{\sum_{i}P(Y_{n+r+1}^{N}\mid Z_{n}=i,Y_{n+r})P(Z_{n}=i\mid Y_{1}^{n+r})}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\max_{i}P(Y_{n+r+1}^{N}\mid Z_{n+r}=i,Y_{n+r})}{\min_{i}P(Y_{n+r+1}^{N}\mid Z_{n+r}=i,Y_{n+r})}\leq\zeta^{-2}.

(D.19)

Plug the results (
D.17
)– (
D.19
) back into (
D.16
) and we can conclude that

\displaystyle\left|\frac{P(Y_{n+1}^{n+r}\mid Y_{n-r}^{n})P(Y_{n+r+1}^{N}\mid Y_{n+1}^{n+r})}{P(Y_{n+1}^{N}\mid Y_{0}^{n})}-1\right|\leq 4\zeta^{-4}k(1-\zeta\pi_{\min})^{r-1}\left\{\zeta^{-2}(1-\zeta\pi_{\min})\vee 1\right\}.

Case 3
:
n+r>N
,
n-r>0
. In this case, we can express the left hand side of (
D.15
) as

\displaystyle\left|\frac{P(Y_{n+1}^{N}\mid Y_{n-r}^{n})}{P(Y_{n+1}^{N}\mid Y_{0}^{n})}-1\right|

\displaystyle=\frac{\left|P(Y_{n+1}^{N}\mid Y_{n-r}^{n})-P(Y_{n+1}^{N}\mid Y_{0}^{n})\right|}{P(Y_{n+1}^{N}\mid Y_{0}^{n})}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\max_{i}P(Y_{n+1}^{N}\mid Z_{n}=i,Y_{n})}{\min_{i}P(Y_{n+1}^{N}\mid Z_{n}=i,Y_{n})}\sum_{i}\left|P(Z_{n}=i\mid Y_{n-r}^{n})-P(Z_{n}=i\mid Y_{0}^{n})\right|

\displaystyle\leq 2\zeta^{-4}k(1-\zeta\pi_{\min})^{r}.

Case 4
:
n+r<N
,
n-r<0
. In this case, we can express the left hand side of (
D.15
) as

\displaystyle\left|\frac{P(Y_{0}^{n+r})P(Y_{n+1}^{N})}{P(Y_{n+1}^{n+r})P(Y_{0}^{N})}-1\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\frac{P(Y_{n+r+1}^{N}\mid Y_{n+1}^{n+r})}{P(Y_{n+r+1}^{N}\mid Y_{0}^{n+r})}-1\right|

\displaystyle=\frac{\left|P(Y_{n+r+1}^{N}\mid Y_{n+1}^{n+r})-P(Y_{n+r+1}^{N}\mid Y_{0}^{n+r})\right|}{P(Y_{n+r+1}^{N}\mid Y_{0}^{n+r})}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\max_{i}P(Y_{n+r+1}^{N}\mid Z_{n+r}=i,Y_{n+r})}{\min_{i}P(Y_{n+r+1}^{N}\mid Z_{n+r}=i,Y_{n+r})}

\displaystyle\quad\times\sum_{i}\left|P(Z_{n+r}=i\mid Y_{n+1}^{n+r})-P(Z_{n+r}=i\mid Y_{0}^{n+r})\right|

\displaystyle\leq 2\zeta^{-4}k(1-\zeta\pi_{\min})^{r-1}.

Combining the results from
Case 1–4
, we can conclude (
D.15
).
∎

Appendix E
Proof Sketch of one-step update

In this section, we show the contraction of the distance between the estimated parameter to the true parameter via one update of EM algorithm. In Section
E.1
, we discuss the one-step update of
\theta_{i}^{\ell}
; in Section
E.2
, we discuss the one-step update of
\sigma^{2}
; in Section
E.3
, we discuss the one-step update of
Q
.

E.1
Proof of Lemma
4.3

Recall the notation introduced in Section
4.3
.
The quantity of interest is
\Delta=\widehat{\theta}-\theta^{\star}
and
{Y}_{n}^{\Delta}=Y_{n,i}-{Y}_{n-1,i}
.
In the following, we show the contraction of
\Delta
at each iterate of the EM algorithm.
By construction, it follows that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\widehat{w}_{\Theta,\ell}(t_{n})\left\{{Y}_{n}^{\Delta}-\theta\widehat{\Psi}(t_{n})\right\}^{2}+\lambda\|\theta\|_{1,\widehat{K}_{\Psi}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\widehat{w}_{\Theta,\ell}(t_{n})\left\{{Y}_{n}^{\Delta}-\theta^{\star}\widehat{\Psi}(t_{n})\right\}^{2}+\lambda\|\theta^{\star}\|_{1,\widehat{K}_{\Psi}}.

By rearranging the above equation, we arrive at
