.

Proof of Lemma
H.1
.

Define
C_{j}
for
j=0,\ldots,9
be some universal constants.
Before the start, we define a quantity

\displaystyle\delta_{e_{0}}=\max\left\{N\exp(-C_{0}ms\log p),6\exp(-C_{1}m\log p)\right\}.

(H.5)

Step 1. The deviation bound and restricted eigenvalue.
To apply Lemma
4.3
, we want to check Assumption
6
holds.
Note that since
\delta_{1}\leq{c_{\Psi}}({4BDm})^{-1}
, then by Lemma
H.3
,
we have

\max_{j}\sigma_{\min}(\widehat{K}_{\Psi_{j}})\geq\frac{c_{\Psi}}{2},\quad\max_{j}\sigma_{\max}(\widehat{K}_{\Psi_{j}})\leq 2c_{\Psi}^{-1}.

(H.6)

This implies that

\max_{j}\frac{\sigma_{\max}(\widehat{K}_{\Psi_{j}})}{\sigma_{\min}(\widehat{K}_{\Psi_{j}})}\leq 4c_{\Psi}^{-2}.

(H.7)

With the above result and under the sample size assumption that

N\gtrsim\{m^{4}(\log p)^{3}\vee m^{5/2}s^{5/2}\}
, we can apply Lemma
G.1
. Then, it follows that Assumption
6
holds with probability at least
1-\delta_{e_{0}}
.

Next, to verify Assumption
7
, we want to show that each of the term
\|\Delta_{w}\|_{\infty,\widehat{K}_{\Psi}^{*}}
,
\|\Delta_{\varepsilon}\|_{\infty,\widehat{K}_{\Psi}^{*}}
,
\|\theta^{\star}\Delta_{\Psi}\|_{\infty,\widehat{K}_{\Psi}^{*}}
is well controlled.
Lemma
E.3
implies that

\|\Delta_{w}\|_{\infty,\widehat{K}_{\Psi}^{*}}\leq C\sqrt{m}\left\{1-\zeta\pi^{2}_{\min}\right\}^{r-1}.

Lemma
E.4
implies that

\|\theta^{\star}\Delta_{\Psi}\|_{\infty,\widehat{K}_{\Psi}^{*}}\leq\frac{4}{c_{\Psi}}\|\theta^{\star}\|_{2}BDm\delta_{1}\sqrt{s}.

Lastly, Lemma
F.1
, implies that

\|\Delta_{\varepsilon}\|_{\infty,\widehat{K}_{\Psi}^{*}}\leq\frac{C\sqrt{m}B(\sigma+\rho)}{c_{\Psi}}\sqrt{\frac{m\log p}{N}},

with probability at least
1-\delta_{e_{0}}
. Therefore, we
can choose

\displaystyle\mathbb{Q}(N,p,s,m,r,\delta_{1})=C_{2}\sqrt{m}\max\left\{\left(1-\zeta\pi_{\min}^{2}\right)^{r-1},\sqrt{ms}\delta_{1},\sqrt{\frac{m\log p}{N}}\right\}.

Note that
\mathbb{Q}
becomes small if we (i) increase
r
, (ii) have small enough
\delta_{1}
, which is the error of the nonparametric regression and depends on the sample size
N
, and (iii) have large enough sample size
N
. We can then
conclude that
\mathbb{Q}
is well controlled.

Step 2. Selecting
\lambda
.
With the results in the previous step, we are able to select
\lambda
as

\lambda\geq\sqrt{m}\max\left\{C_{2}\left(1-\zeta\pi_{\min}^{2}\right)^{r-1},\sqrt{ms}C_{2}\delta_{1},C_{2}\sqrt{\frac{m\log p}{N}},\frac{C_{3}}{\sqrt{ms}}\max_{i,\ell}\|\check{\theta}_{i\cdot}^{\ell}-\theta_{i\cdot}^{\ell\star}\|\right\},

where
C_{2},C_{3}>0
are some universal constants.
Apply Lemma
4.3
and (
H.6
) , we have

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\theta}_{i\cdot}^{\ell}-\theta_{i\cdot}^{\ell\star}\|_{2}\leq\frac{4c_{\Psi}^{-1}}{\alpha}\left(10\lambda\sqrt{s}+\|\check{\theta}_{i\cdot}^{\ell}-\theta_{i\cdot}^{\ell\star}\|_{2}\right),

(H.8)

with probability at least
1-2\delta_{e_{0}}
and
\alpha=2^{-1}\sigma_{\min}\left(\mathbb{E}\left[N^{-1}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\widehat{w}_{\Theta,\ell}(t_{n})\Psi(t_{n})\Psi(t_{n})^{\top}\right]\right)
. Take the maximum over
i=1,\ldots,p
, then

\max_{i,\ell}\|\widehat{\theta}_{i\cdot}^{\ell}-\theta_{i\cdot}^{\ell\star}\|_{2}\leq\frac{4c_{\Psi}^{-1}}{\alpha}\left(10\lambda\sqrt{s}+\max_{i,\ell}\|\check{\theta}_{i\cdot}^{\ell}-\theta_{i\cdot}^{\ell\star}\|_{2}\right),

with probability at least
1-2pk\delta_{e_{0}}
.

Step 3. Combining results.

By definition of
\lambda
, we can bound the term
10\lambda\sqrt{s}
as

\displaystyle 10\lambda\sqrt{s}\leq\max\left\{C_{4}\sqrt{ms}(1-\zeta\pi_{\min}^{2})^{r-1},C_{4}ms\delta_{1},C_{4}m\sqrt{\frac{s\log p}{N}},C_{5}\max_{i,\ell}\|\check{\theta}_{i\cdot}^{\ell}-\theta_{i\cdot}^{\ell\star}\|\right\}.

Therefore, plug the above equation to (
H.8
), we can conclude that

\max_{i,\ell}\|\widehat{\theta}_{i\cdot}^{\ell}-\theta_{i\cdot}^{\ell\star}\|_{2}\\
\leq\frac{4c_{\Psi}^{-1}}{\alpha}\left\{C_{4}\left(\sqrt{ms}(1-\zeta\pi_{\min}^{2})^{r-1},+ms\delta_{1}+m\sqrt{\frac{s\log p}{N}}\right)+(C_{5}+1)\max_{i,\ell}\|\check{\theta}_{i\cdot}^{\ell}-\theta_{i\cdot}^{\ell\star}\|_{2}\right\},

with probability at least
1-2pk\delta_{e_{0}}
.

Apply Lemma
4.2
and under Assumption
10
, we can further obtain

\max_{i,\ell}\|\widehat{\theta}^{\ell}_{i\cdot}-\theta_{i\cdot}^{\ell\star}\|_{2}\leq{C_{6}c_{\Psi}^{-2}}\kappa\max_{i,\ell}\|{\theta}^{\ell}_{i\cdot}-\theta_{i\cdot}^{\ell\star}\|_{2}+C_{7}\left\{\frac{m\sqrt{s\log p}}{\sqrt{N}}+ms\delta_{1}+\sqrt{ms}\left(1-\zeta^{2}\pi_{\min}^{2}\right)^{r-1}\right\},

with probability at least
1-2k\max\{N\exp(-C_{8}ms\log p),\exp(-C_{9}m\log p)\}
.
∎

H.3
Proof of Lemma
H.2

Lemma H.2
.

Under Assumption
2
,
3
,
5
,
8
–
10
.
Suppose that
Z(0)
is sampled from the stationary distribution.
Suppose that
N\gtrsim\left\{m^{4}(\log p)^{3}\vee m^{5/2}s^{5/2}\right\}
,

\max_{i}|\!|\!|\widehat{X}_{i}-X_{i}|\!|\!|=\delta_{1}\leq c_{\Psi}(4BDm)^{-1}
and
C_{0},\ldots,C_{2}>0
be some constants.
If
\sum_{i,j}\|\theta_{ij}^{\ell}-\theta_{ij}^{\ell\star}\|_{2}^{2}\leq r_{0}^{2}
for
\ell=1,\ldots,k
, we have

\left|\widehat{\sigma}^{2}-\sigma^{\star 2}\right|\leq\frac{C_{0}c_{\Psi}^{-2}msr_{0}^{2}}{\sqrt{N}}+C_{1}k\zeta^{-8}\pi_{\min}^{-2}\left\{1-(\zeta\pi_{\min})^{2}\right\}^{r-1}+\left|\check{\sigma}^{2}-\sigma^{\star 2}\right|,

with probability at least
1-Npk\exp(-C_{2})
.

Proof of Lemma
H.2
.

We want to verify that there exist a valid constant
c_{0}
such that
R_{n,\ell},\widehat{R}_{n,\ell}
defined in (
E.9
)
satisfy
R_{n,\ell},\widehat{R}_{n,\ell}\leq c_{0}^{2}p
for
\ell=1,\ldots,k
and
n=1,\ldots,N
. Then, we can apply Lemma
E.1
. It suffices to show that

\displaystyle\max_{i,n,\ell}\left|Y_{n,i}-Y_{n-1,i}-\sum_{j}\theta_{ij}^{\ell}\Psi_{j}(t_{n})\right|\leq c_{0};

\displaystyle\max_{i,n,\ell}\left|Y_{n,i}-Y_{n-1,i}-\sum_{j}\theta_{ij}^{\ell}\widehat{\Psi}_{j}(t_{n})\right|\leq c_{0}.

Note that we can decompose

Y_{n,i}-Y_{n-1,i}-\sum_{j}\theta_{ij}^{\ell}\Psi_{j}(t_{n})=\varepsilon_{n,i}-\varepsilon_{n-1,i}+\rho_{n,i}-\sum_{j}(\theta_{ij}^{\ell}-\theta_{ij}^{\ell\star})\Psi_{j}(t_{n}).

The first two variables are independent centered Gaussian random variables with variance
2\sigma^{\star 2}
. The third variable is
\rho_{n,i}
is the bias induced by approximation and is bounded by
\rho
. Under the assumptions,
(
H.7
) holds. Hence, we can upper bound the last term of the above equation using Hölder inequality as

\left|\sum_{j}(\theta_{ij}^{\ell}-\theta_{ij}^{\ell\star})\Psi_{j}(t_{n})\right|\leq\|\theta^{\ell}_{i\cdot}-\theta_{i\cdot}^{\ell\star}\|_{1}\|\Psi_{j}(t_{n})\|_{\infty}\leq 20c_{\Psi}^{-2}\sqrt{ms}r_{0}B,

where the last inequality follows from plugging (
H.7
) into (
E.12
). Similarly, we have

\left|\sum_{j}(\theta_{ij}^{\ell}-\theta_{ij}^{\ell\star})\widehat{\Psi}_{j}(t_{n})\right|\leq 20c_{\Psi}^{-2}\sqrt{ms}r_{0}B.

Then, there exist a valid finite constant
c_{0}=C_{1}\sigma^{\star}+\rho+20c_{\Psi}^{-2}\sqrt{ms}r_{0}B
for some universally constant
C_{1}
such that

\displaystyle P

\displaystyle\left(\left|Y_{n,i}-Y_{n-1,i}-\sum_{j}\theta_{ij}^{\ell}\Psi_{j}(t_{n})\right|<c_{0}\right)\geq\exp(-C_{2});

\displaystyle P

\displaystyle\left(\left|Y_{n,i}-Y_{n-1,i}-\sum_{j}\theta_{ij}^{\ell}\widehat{\Psi}_{j}(t_{n})\right|<c_{0}\right)\geq\exp(-C_{2}).

Applying the union bound over all
\ell,i,n
, we have

\displaystyle P

\displaystyle\left(\max_{i,n,\ell}\left|Y_{n,i}-Y_{n-1,i}-\sum_{j}\theta_{ij}^{\ell}\Psi_{j}(t_{n})\right|>c_{0}\right)\leq 1-Npk\exp(-C_{2});

\displaystyle P

\displaystyle\left(\max_{i,n,\ell}\left|Y_{n,i}-Y_{n-1,i}-\sum_{j}\theta_{ij}^{\ell}\widehat{\Psi}_{j}(t_{n})\right|>c_{0}\right)\leq 1-Npk\exp(-C_{2}).

Along with this piece of result and Lemma
E.6
, we can apply Lemma
E.1
and arrive at

\displaystyle\left|\widehat{\sigma}^{2}-\sigma^{\star 2}\right|\leq\frac{C_{0}\sqrt{s}kc^{-1}_{\Psi}}{NBD\sqrt{m}}+\frac{\left(\sigma^{\star}\right)^{2}+\rho^{2}+c_{\Psi}^{-2}msr_{0}^{2}B^{2}}{\sqrt{N}}+C_{1}k\zeta^{-8}\pi_{\min}^{-2}\left\{1-(\zeta\pi_{\min})^{2}\right\}^{r-1}+\left|\check{\sigma}^{2}-\sigma^{\star 2}\right|,

with probability at least
1-Npk\exp(-C_{2})
. Note that the second term of the above equation dominates the first term, and hence we can simplify the above results as

\left|\widehat{\sigma}^{2}-\sigma^{\star 2}\right|\leq\frac{C_{0}c_{\Psi}^{-2}msr_{0}^{2}}{\sqrt{N}}+C_{1}k\zeta^{-8}\pi_{\min}^{-2}\left\{1-(\zeta\pi_{\min})^{2}\right\}^{r-1}+\left|\check{\sigma}^{2}-\sigma^{\star 2}\right|,

(H.9)

where
C_{0}
is a constant that depends on
\sigma^{\star},\rho,B
.

∎

Lemma H.3

(Adapted from Lemma 3 in
Chen et al. (2017)
)
.

Suppose that Assumption
10
holds, and
\max_{i}|\!|\!|\widehat{X}_{i}-X_{i}|\!|\!|=\max_{i}\{\int_{0}^{1}(\widehat{X}_{i}(t)-X_{i}(t))^{2}\mathrm{d}t\}^{1/2}=\delta_{1}
. Assume that
|g_{j}(X_{i}(t))|\leq B
and

|g^{\prime}_{j}(X_{i}(t))|\leq D

for
i=1,\ldots,p
and
j=1,\ldots,m
and
t\in[0,1]
. Then,

c_{\Psi}-2BDm\delta_{1}\leq\sigma_{\min}\left(\widehat{K}_{\Psi_{j}}\right)\leq\sigma_{\max}\left(\widehat{K}_{\Psi_{j}}\right)\leq c_{\Psi}^{-1}+2BDm\delta_{1}.

Appendix I
Useful Lemmas

Lemma I.1

(Lemma 5 in
Wong et al. (2020)
)
.

Let
X
be a random variable. Then the following statements are equivalent for every
\gamma>0
. The constants
K_{1}
,
K_{2}
,
K_{3}
differ from each other at most by a constant depending only on
\gamma
.

1.

The tails of
X
satisfy

P(|X|>t)\leq 2\exp\{-(t/K_{1})^{\gamma}\},\quad t\geq 0.

2.

The moments of
X
satisfy

\|X\|_{p}:=\left(\mathbb{E}|X|^{p}\right)^{1/p}\leq K_{2}p^{1/\gamma},\quad p\geq\min(1,\gamma).

3.

The moment generating function of
|X|^{\gamma}
is finite:

\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(\left|X\right|/K_{3}\right)^{\gamma}\right]\leq 2.

Lemma I.2

(Adapted from Lemma 13 in
Wong et al. (2020)
and Theorem 1 in
Merlevède et al. (2011)
)
.

Let
\{X_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a strictly stationary sequence of zero mean random variables that are sub-Weibull(
\gamma_{2}
) with sub-Weibull norm
K
. Suppose that
\{X_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is
\beta
-mixing with coefficients satisfying
\beta(n)\leq 2\exp(-cn^{\gamma_{1}})
. Let
S_{N}=\sum_{n=1}^{N}X_{n}
and define
1/\gamma=1/\gamma_{1}+1/\gamma_{2}
. Assume
\gamma<1
. Then for
N>4
and any
t>1/N
,

P\left(\left|N^{-1}S_{N}\right|>t\right)\leq N\exp\left(-\frac{(tN)^{\gamma}}{K^{\gamma}C_{1}}\right)+\exp\left(-\frac{t^{2}N}{K^{2}C_{2}}\right),

where the constants
C_{1},C_{2}
depend only on
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}
and
c
.

Definition 16
.

For every
\gamma>0
, the sub-Weibull norm is defined as

\|X\|_{\psi_{\gamma}}:=\sup_{p\geq 1}(\mathbb{E}|X|^{p})^{1/p}p^{-1/\gamma}.

Lemma I.3
.

Let
X
be a sub-Weibull(
\alpha
) random variable and
Y
be a sub-Weibull(
\beta
) random variable. Let
1/\gamma=1/\alpha+1/\beta
. Then
XY
is a sub-Weibull(
\gamma
) random variable with sub-Weibull norm bounded by

\|XY\|_{\psi_{\gamma}}\leq\left(\frac{\alpha+\beta}{\beta}\right)^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}\left(\frac{\alpha+\beta}{\alpha}\right)^{\frac{1}{\beta}}\|X\|_{\psi_{\alpha}}\|Y\|_{\psi_{\beta}}.

Proof.

First, by Hölder inequality, we have

\mathbb{E}|XY|^{p}\leq\left(\mathbb{E}|X|^{p\frac{\alpha}{\gamma}}\right)^{\frac{\gamma}{\alpha}}\left(\mathbb{E}|Y|^{p\frac{\beta}{\gamma}}\right)^{\frac{\gamma}{\beta}}.

By taking root-
p
on both side, we obtain the inequality

\|XY\|_{p}\leq\|X\|_{p\frac{\alpha}{\gamma}}\|Y\|_{p\frac{\beta}{\gamma}}.

By definition of the sub-Weibull norm, we can further bound the above term as

\|XY\|_{p}\leq\|X\|_{\psi_{\alpha}}\left(\frac{\alpha}{\gamma}\right)^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}p^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}\|Y\|_{\psi_{\beta}}\left(\frac{\beta}{\gamma}\right)^{\frac{1}{\beta}}p^{\frac{1}{\beta}}=p^{\frac{1}{\gamma}}\left(\frac{\alpha+\beta}{\beta}\right)^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}\left(\frac{\alpha+\beta}{\alpha}\right)^{\frac{1}{\beta}}\|X\|_{\psi_{\alpha}}\|Y\|_{\psi_{\beta}}.

Hence

\|XY\|_{\psi_{\gamma}}=\sup_{p\geq 1}p^{-\frac{1}{\gamma}}\|XY\|_{p}\leq\left(\frac{\alpha+\beta}{\beta}\right)^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}\left(\frac{\alpha+\beta}{\alpha}\right)^{\frac{1}{\beta}}\|X\|_{\psi_{\alpha}}\|Y\|_{\psi_{\beta}}.

∎

Lemma I.4
.

Let
X
be a sub-Weibull(
\gamma
) random variable, we have

\|\mathbb{E}X\|_{\psi_{\gamma}}\leq\|X\|_{\psi_{\gamma}}

Proof of Lemma
I.4
.

Write

\displaystyle\|\mathbb{E}X\|_{\psi_{\gamma}}

\displaystyle\leq\left|\mathbb{E}X\right|

\displaystyle=\|X\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\|X\|_{\psi_{\gamma}}.

∎

Lemma I.5

(Theorem 5.1 in
Bradley (2005)
)
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{A}_{n}
,
\mathcal{B}_{n}
for
n\in\mathbb{N}
are
\sigma
-fields, The
\sigma
-fields
\sigma(\{\mathcal{A}_{n},\mathcal{B}_{n}\})
for
n\in\mathbb{N}
are independent.Then,

\beta\left(\sigma(\{\mathcal{A}_{n}:{n\in\mathbb{N}}\}),\sigma(\{\mathcal{B}_{n}:{n\in\mathbb{N}}\})\right)\leq\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\beta(\mathcal{A}_{n},\mathcal{B}_{n}).

Lemma I.6

(Measurable Mapping of Stationary Process)
.

Let
(\mathcal{X},\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{X}))
and
(\mathcal{Y},\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{Y}))
be two measurable spaces and
\mathscr{B}(\cdot)
denotes the Borel
\sigma
-field. Let

(X_{t}\in\mathcal{X})_{t\geq 0}
be a strictly stationary process and
g:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
be a Borel measurable function. Then,
(g(X_{t})\in\mathcal{Y})_{t\geq 0}
is strictly stationary.

Proof of Lemma
I.6
.

A stochastic process is called strictly stationary if for any finite set of random variables,
X_{t_{1}},\ldots,X_{t_{k}}
, from the stochastic process, the following equation is satisfied

\displaystyle(X_{t_{1}},\ldots,X_{t_{k}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{d}}}{{=}}(X_{t_{1}+\tau},\ldots,X_{t_{k}+\tau}),\quad\text{for all }\tau\in\mathbb{R}.

That is, the joint distribution of any finite set of random variables are invariant to times shifts.

Let
Y_{t}=g(X_{t})
and write

\displaystyle P(Y_{t_{1}}\in B_{1},\ldots,Y_{t_{k}}\in B_{k})

\displaystyle=P(X_{t_{1}}\in g^{-1}(B_{1}),\ldots,X_{t_{k}}\in g^{-1}(B_{k}))

\displaystyle=P(X_{t_{1}+\tau}\in g^{-1}(B_{1}),\ldots,X_{t_{k}+\tau}\in g^{-1}(B_{k}))

\displaystyle=P(Y_{t_{1}+\tau}\in B_{1},\ldots,Y_{t_{k}+\tau}\in B_{k}).

Therefore, we complete the proof.
∎

Lemma I.7

(Strictly Stationary Markov Process)
.

Suppose that
(X_{t}\in\mathcal{X})_{t\geq 0}
is a time-homogeneous Markov process with stationary distribution
\pi
. Let
x_{0}
be the initial point sampled from the stationary distribution
\pi
. Then, the stochastic process
(X_{t})_{t\geq 0}
with
X_{0}=x_{0}
is a strictly stationary process. Additionally, let
g:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
be a Borel measurable function. Define the stochastic process
(Y_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
as
Y_{n}=\int_{t_{n-1}}^{t_{n}}g(X_{u})\mathrm{d}u
, where
t_{n}-t_{n-1}=h
for some fixed
h>0
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Then,
(Y_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a strictly stationary process.

Proof of Lemma
I.7
.

To show the first statement, we use the Markov property. Without the loss of generality, assume
t_{1}\leq\cdots\leq t_{k}
. Then,

\displaystyle P(X_{t_{1}},\ldots,X_{t_{k}})

\displaystyle=P(X_{t_{k}}\mid X_{t_{k-1}})P(X_{t_{k-1}}\mid X_{t_{k-2}})\cdots P(X_{t_{2}}\mid X_{t_{1}})P(X_{t_{1}})

\displaystyle=P(X_{t_{k}+\tau}\mid X_{t_{k-1+\tau}})P(X_{t_{k-1}+\tau}\mid X_{t_{k-2}+\tau})\cdots P(X_{t_{2}+\tau}\mid X_{t_{1}+\tau})P(X_{t_{1}}).

Since
P(X_{0}){=}\pi
, we have
P(X_{t_{1}}){=}P(X_{t_{1}+\tau}){=}\pi
, therefore we can write the above term as

\displaystyle=P(X_{t_{k}+\tau}\mid X_{t_{k-1+\tau}})P(X_{t_{k-1}+\tau}\mid X_{t_{k-2}+\tau})\cdots P(X_{t_{2}+\tau}\mid X_{t_{1}+\tau})P(X_{t_{1}+\tau})

\displaystyle=P(X_{t_{1}+\tau},\ldots,X_{t_{k}+\tau}).

To show the second statement, we first condition on
X_{t_{0}}=x
and write

\displaystyle P(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{k}\mid X_{t_{0}}=x)

\displaystyle=P\left(\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}X_{u}\mathrm{d}u,\ldots,\int_{t_{k-1}}^{t_{k}}X_{u}\mathrm{d}u\;\bigg|\;X_{t_{0}}=x\right).

Apply the time-homogeneous Markov property, we can write the above term as

\displaystyle=P\left(\int_{t_{s}}^{t_{s+1}}X_{u}\mathrm{d}u,\ldots,\int_{t_{s+k-1}}^{t_{s+k}}X_{u}\mathrm{d}u\;\bigg|\;X_{t_{s}}=x\right)

\displaystyle=P(Y_{s+1},\ldots,Y_{s+k}\mid X_{t_{s}}=x)

Since
P(X_{t_{0}})=P(X_{t_{s}})
, we can write

P(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{k}\mid X_{t_{0}})P(X_{t_{0}})=P(Y_{s+1},\ldots,Y_{s+k}\mid X_{t_{s}})P(X_{t_{s}}).

Marginalize both side, we obtain

P(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{k})=P(Y_{s+1},\ldots,Y_{s+k}).

∎

Lemma I.8

(Lemma 12 in
Loh and Wainwright (2012)
)
.

Denote the set
\mathbb{K}(s)=\{v\in\mathbb{R}^{p}:\|v\|_{0}\leq s,\|v\|_{2}\leq 1\}
. Let
A\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times p}
be a fixed matrix,
\delta>0
be the tolerance. Suppose that

\left|\nu^{\top}A\nu\right|\leq\delta\quad\forall\nu\in\mathbb{K}(2s).

Then

\left|\nu^{\top}A\nu\right|\leq 27\delta\left(\|\nu\|_{2}^{2}+s^{-1}\|\nu\|_{1}^{2}\right).
